Brownian Kernel Ladders
Mahdi Mohammadigohari Mahdi.Mohammadigohari@gmail.com, Giuseppe Di Fatta giuseppe.difatta@unibz.it, Giuseppe Nicosia giuseppe.nicosia@unict.it, Panos M Pardalos pardalos@ise.ufl.edu

Abstract
Constructing mathematically tractable hierarchical function spaces capable of encoding compositional representations underlying deep learning models remains a central challenge in statistical learning theory. While reproducing kernel Hilbert spaces (RKHSs) provide powerful nonparametric models with strong analytical and statistical properties, classical RKHS constructions are inherently shallow and do not naturally generate hierarchical function-space structures. In this work, we introduce Brownian kernel ladders (BKLs), a recursively defined hierarchy of integral RKHSs generated through Brownian-kernel integral constructions. Starting from linear functionals, each layer is constructed recursively by integrating Brownian kernels over probability measures supported on subsets of the previous layer, yielding a hierarchy in which depth is encoded directly through the underlying recursive function-space construction. Based on this framework, we define canonical BKL spaces together with an associated complexity functional. We establish several analytical and statistical properties of the resulting function classes. In particular, we prove that BKL spaces form quasi-Banach spaces, satisfy depth-dependent Hölder regularity estimates, and exhibit strict monotonicity of the associated function-space hierarchy with respect to depth. We further establish existence results for regularized empirical risk minimization over BKL spaces and derive Gaussian complexity bounds for balls in BKL spaces that remain uniformly controlled with respect to both the ambient dimension and the hierarchy depth. A key ingredient of the analysis is a new combinatorial proof technique based on recursive subset decompositions and threshold representations associated with the Brownian kernel. These estimates yield excess-risk guarantees of near-parametric order for regularized empirical risk minimization over BKL spaces. Our results provide a mathematically tractable hierarchical function-space framework for studying compositional representations underlying deep learning models, in which increasing depth yields strictly larger hypothesis classes together with uniformly controlled statistical complexity behavior.

1
Introduction

Deep neural networks exhibit hierarchical compositional structure and achieve remarkable empirical success across a broad range of applications
(
22
;
15
)
. From a function-space perspective, this hierarchical behavior naturally motivates recursive constructions in which increasingly expressive function classes are generated layer-by-layer from previous levels. However, mathematically tractable recursive function-space models that simultaneously admit explicit analytical structure, increasing expressivity with depth, and statistically stable behavior remain comparatively limited. In particular, many classical nonparametric learning frameworks are based on reproducing kernel Hilbert spaces (RKHSs), which provide analytically tractable hypothesis classes together with powerful approximation and generalization guarantees. Standard RKHS constructions, however, are inherently shallow and do not naturally generate hierarchical function-space structures.

In this work, we introduce a new hierarchical kernel framework called
Brownian kernel ladders
(BKLs). The proposed construction is based on recursive integral RKHSs driven by the Brownian kernel

\displaystyle k^{(\mathrm{B})}\left(x,x^{\prime}\right)=\frac{|x|+|x^{\prime}|-|x-x^{\prime}|}{2},\qquad\text{for all }x,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}.

Starting from linear functionals, each layer is generated recursively by integrating Brownian kernels over probability measures supported on subsets of the previous layer. This procedure produces a hierarchy of recursively generated RKHSs in which depth is encoded through the underlying recursive function-space construction rather than through a finite-dimensional network parameterization.

A central feature of the proposed construction is the role of the Brownian kernel. Its non-negative
1
-homogeneous structure induces recursive Hölder-type regularity across the hierarchy and leads to favorable statistical-complexity behavior. In contrast to many recursive compositional constructions whose complexity deteriorates with increasing depth, the proposed framework permits dimension-independent statistical guarantees together with uniformly controlled dependence on the depth parameter. More precisely, we show that the resulting hierarchical spaces exhibit strictly increasing expressivity across depth while maintaining Gaussian complexity and excess-risk bounds of order
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(n^{-1/2}\right)
uniformly over all hierarchy levels.

Recent years have witnessed growing interaction between kernel methods and deep learning through deep kernel constructions, convolutional kernel models, neural tangent kernels, and related hierarchical function-space formulations
(
10
;
32
;
21
;
24
;
16
)
. Recent work has explored recursive and compositional kernel constructions in deep kernel learning
(
19
)
. In these approaches, hierarchical structure is generated through successive transformations of base kernels, and the resulting analysis is centered primarily on the induced RKHSs and their inclusion and expressivity properties as the depth level increases.

Related function-space viewpoints on deep learning also appear in reproducing kernel Banach space (RKBS), Barron-space, and generalized Barron-space frameworks. Deep neural RKBS constructions
(
6
)
provide Banach-space interpretations of deep neural architectures through compositions of vector-valued integral RKBS layers. In this setting, functions admit representations of the form

\displaystyle f(x)=\int_{\Theta}\rho(x,\theta)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(\theta),\qquad\mu\in\mathcal{M}(\Theta,\mathcal{Y}),

where
\Theta
is a parameter space,
\mathcal{Y}
is a Banach space,
\rho:\mathcal{X}\times\Theta\to\mathbb{R}
is a basis function, and
\mathcal{M}(\Theta,\mathcal{Y})
denotes the Banach space of
\mathcal{Y}
-valued Radon measures of bounded variation. Deep RKBSs are then generated through recursive compositions of such integral Banach-space layers, leading to variational formulations and representer theorems for finite-width deep architectures. A related recursive Banach-space perspective is developed in the chain reproducing kernel Banach space (cRKBS) framework of
17
. In this approach, deep neural networks are modeled through recursive compositions of kernels rather than compositions of functions, leading to recursive kernel-chain RKBS constructions. The resulting function classes admit recursive representations of the form

\displaystyle f^{(L)}(x)=\int_{\Omega^{(L)}}\sigma^{(L)}\left(f^{(L-1)}(x)+b\right)\beta^{(L)}\left(f^{(L-1)},b\right)\,\mathrm{d}\mu\left(f^{(L-1)},b\right),

where
x\in\mathcal{X}
is an input point,
\Omega^{(L)}\subseteq\mathcal{F}^{(L-1)}\times\mathbb{R}
is the parameter domain associated with the
L
-th layer,
\mathcal{F}^{(L-1)}
denotes the corresponding Banach space of functions from the previous layer,
f^{(L-1)}\in\mathcal{F}^{(L-1)}
is a previous-layer function,
b\in\mathbb{R}
is a bias parameter,
\sigma^{(L)}
is an activation function,
\beta^{(L)}
is a weighting function, and
\mu
is a Radon measure on
\Omega^{(L)}
. The framework establishes correspondences between neural cRKBS functions and finite deep neural networks on finite datasets. In contrast to the present work, the emphasis of these RKBS-based approaches lies primarily on variational formulations, duality structure, and representer-theorem characterizations of deep neural architectures, rather than on analytical properties of recursively generated function-space hierarchies and their statistical complexity behavior across depth.

Broader infinite-width function-space perspectives also arise in classical Barron-space theory
(
2
)
, where infinitely wide two-layer neural-network models are represented through integral representations of the form

\displaystyle f(\mathbf{x})=\int_{\Omega}a\,\sigma\left(\bm{\omega}^{\top}\mathbf{x}+c\right)\,\mathrm{d}\rho(a,\bm{\omega},c),\qquad\Omega=\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R},

with
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\sigma:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
an activation function, and
\rho
a measure over network parameters. A different recursive Banach-space construction was later developed by
12
, where hierarchical spaces are generated through recursive constructions of the form

\displaystyle f(x)=\int_{\mathbb{B}_{1}(X)}\sigma(g(x))\,\mathrm{d}\mu(g),

with
X\subseteq\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{X})
a Banach space of continuous functions on the compact domain
\mathcal{X}
, and
\sigma:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
,
\sigma(u)=\max\{u,0\}
, the ReLU activation. These generalized Barron-space models are primarily developed from approximation-theoretic, optimization, and training-dynamics perspectives for infinitely wide multilayer ReLU networks. Their associated approximation and complexity estimates typically retain explicit deterioration with increasing depth.

More broadly, statistical analyses of recursive compositional models frequently exhibit explicit dependence on the depth parameter through mechanisms such as recursive covering-number estimates
(
5
;
31
)
, pseudo-dimension growth
(
1
;
4
)
, norm propagation
(
26
;
3
)
, or layerwise complexity accumulation
(
14
)
. Such behavior arises both in capacity-based analyses of deep neural networks and in recent deep kernel-learning frameworks
(
33
)
. Even when these dependencies are substantially improved relative to classical deep-network analyses — for example, to mild square-root dependence on the depth parameter — constructing hierarchical function-space models with uniformly controlled statistical complexity across arbitrary depth levels remains challenging.

Based on the hierarchical BKL construction, we introduce canonical Brownian kernel ladder spaces together with an associated complexity functional. We prove that the resulting function classes form quasi-Banach spaces, satisfy depth-dependent Hölder regularity estimates, and exhibit strict monotonicity of the associated function-space hierarchy with respect to the depth parameter, showing that increasing the number of levels strictly enlarges the associated hypothesis class.

Beyond the analytical structure of the hierarchy, we study the statistical properties of learning with BKLs through regularized empirical risk minimization. A central technical component of our analysis is a new Gaussian complexity bounds for balls in BKL spaces. Standard entropy-based methods and chaining techniques
(
5
;
23
)
are not directly applicable in the present setting and lead to suboptimal estimates. Instead, we develop a new combinatorial proof technique based on recursive subset decompositions and threshold representations associated with the Brownian kernel. This approach yields Gaussian complexity bounds of order
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(n^{-1/2}\right)
uniformly with respect to both the ambient dimension and the hierarchy depth. These estimates further imply excess-risk guarantees of the same order for regularized empirical risk minimization over BKL spaces. Consequently, the recursive hierarchical structure of the model does not deteriorate the resulting statistical learning rate.

The proposed framework is also closely related to several recent hierarchical kernel constructions. In the shallow case
L=2
, the BKL construction specializes to an integral kernel of the form

\displaystyle k\left[\mathbb{S}^{d-1},\hat{\mu}\right]\left(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\right)=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}k^{(\mathrm{B})}\left(\bm{\omega}^{\top}\mathbf{x},\bm{\omega}^{\top}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\hat{\mu}\left(\bm{\omega}\right),

where
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
denotes the unit sphere,
\bm{\omega}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
is a projection direction,
\hat{\mu}
is a probability measure on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, and
k^{(\mathrm{B})}
denotes the Brownian kernel. This coincides with the Brownian projection kernel used in Brownian kernel-enhanced random neural networks (BKERNNs)
(
13
)
. More generally, BKLs share structural similarities with neural Hilbert ladders (NHLs)
(
9
)
, which are likewise based on recursively generated integral RKHS constructions. However, the recursive integral kernels underlying the two hierarchies are fundamentally different. In NHLs, the hierarchy is generated through recursive activation-based integral kernel constructions of the form

\displaystyle k^{(l+1)}\left[\mathcal{H}^{(l)},\mu^{(l)}\right](\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime})=\int_{\mathcal{H}^{(l)}}\sigma\left(u(\mathbf{x})\right)\sigma\left(u\left(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}\mu^{(l)}(u),

where
\mathcal{H}^{(l)}
denotes the RKHS associated with the
l
-th level and
\mu^{(l)}
is a probability measure on
\mathcal{H}^{(l)}
. In contrast, in the present work the hierarchy is generated through recursive Brownian-kernel integral constructions of the form

\displaystyle k^{(l)}\left[\mathcal{S}^{(l)},\mu^{(l)}\right](\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime})=\int_{\mathcal{S}^{(l)}}k^{(\mathrm{B})}\left(u(\mathbf{x}),u\left(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}\mu^{(l)}(u),

where
\mathcal{S}^{(l)}
is a measurable subset of the corresponding RKHS and
\mu^{(l)}\in\mathcal{P}_{1}\left(\mathcal{S}^{(l)}\right)
is a probability measure with finite first moment. This distinction leads to substantially different analytical and statistical properties of the associated function classes. In particular, the Gaussian complexity bounds established in the present work remain uniformly controlled with respect to the depth parameter, in contrast to the depth-dependent complexity behavior appearing in existing hierarchical kernel constructions.

Our main contributions can be summarized as follows:

1.

We introduce Brownian kernel ladders (BKLs), a hierarchical framework of recursively generated integral RKHSs constructed through Brownian-kernel integral representations.

2.

We define canonical BKL spaces together with an associated complexity functional.

3.

We establish analytical properties of the resulting spaces, including quasi-Banach structure, depth-dependent Hölder regularity across the hierarchy, and strict monotonicity of the associated function-space hierarchy with respect to depth.

4.

We prove existence results for regularized empirical risk minimization over BKL spaces.

5.

We derive Gaussian complexity bounds for balls in BKL spaces that remain uniformly controlled with respect to both the ambient dimension and the depth level.

6.

Since standard entropy-based and chaining techniques yield suboptimal estimates in the present setting, we develop a new combinatorial proof technique based on recursive subset decompositions and threshold representations associated with the Brownian kernel.

7.

We establish excess-risk guarantees of order
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(n^{-1/2}\right)
for regularized empirical risk minimization over BKL spaces.

The remainder of the paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
, we introduce notation and preliminaries. Section
3
presents the construction of Brownian kernel ladders and the associated complexity spaces. The analytical properties of the resulting hierarchy are established in Section
4.1
. Statistical guarantees, including Gaussian complexity and excess-risk bounds, are presented in Section
4.2
. The proofs of the main results are deferred to the appendices.

Our main theoretical results are gathered in Table

for the readers’ convenience

The set of natural numbers is denoted by
\mathbb{N}\coloneqq\{0,1,2,\ldots\}
;
\mathbb{Z}^{>0}\coloneqq\{1,2,\ldots\}
stands for positive integers; the set of non-negative reals is
\mathbb{R}^{\geq 0}
. For
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{>0}
, let
[n]\coloneqq\{1,\ldots,n\}
. The Cartesian product of sets
(A_{i})_{i\in I}
is written as
\prod_{i\in I}A_{i}
; when the cardinality of the index set
I
is finite (
n\coloneq|I|
) and
A:=A_{1}=\ldots=A_{n}
, we use the shorthand
A^{n}
. For a set
E
, its indicator function is
\mathds{1}_{E}
:

\mathds{1}_{E}(x)=1
if
x\in E
and
\mathds{1}_{E}(x)=0
otherwise. For positive sequences
(a_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
and
(b_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
, we write
a_{n}=\mathcal{O}(b_{n})
if there exist
C>0
and
n_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}^{>0}
such that for all
n\geq n_{0}
one has
a_{n}\leq Cb_{n}
;
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(a_{n})
coincides with
\mathcal{O}(a_{n})
apart from discarding polylogarithmic factors of
n
, in other words
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(a_{n})=\mathcal{O}(a_{n}\mathrm{polylog}(n))
. Let
\mathcal{U}
be a topological space enriched with the Borel sigma-field; we denote the space of probability measures on
\mathcal{U}
by
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{U})
.
Expectation is denoted by
\mathbb{E}
, and
\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}[X]\ =\int_{\mathcal{X}}x\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}(x)
, where
X\sim\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})
.
The
n
-fold product of a probability measure
\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{U})
is denoted by
\mathbb{P}^{n}
. The standard normal distribution on
\mathbb{R}
with mean
0
and variance
1

is denoted by
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
. The Dirac measure concentrated at point
x
is denoted by
\delta_{x}
. For a probability measure
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{U})
,
p\in[1,\infty)
, the Banach space of real-valued
L^{p}
-integrable functions on
\mathcal{U}
w.r.t.
\mu
is denoted by
L^{p}(\mathcal{U},\mu)
, and for
g\in L^{p}(\mathcal{U},\mu)
, we write
\|g\|_{L^{p}(\mathcal{U},\mu)}\coloneqq\left[\int_{\mathcal{U}}|g(u)|^{p}\mathrm{d}\mu(u)\right]^{1/p}
. Let
\mathcal{S}
be a measurable subset of a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
. We denote the class of Borel probability measures on
\mathcal{S}
with finite first moment by
\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{S})\coloneqq\left\{\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S}):\|\mu\|_{\mathcal{S},1}<\infty\right\}
, where

\displaystyle\|\mu\|_{\mathcal{S},1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\int_{\mathcal{S}}\|u\|_{\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{d}\mu(u).

(1)

The set of all Borel probability measures on

\mathcal{S}
with first moment equal to one is denoted by
\mathcal{P}_{1,1}(\mathcal{S})\coloneqq\left\{\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S}):\|\mu\|_{\mathcal{S},1}=1\right\}
. For a vector
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
p\in[1,\infty)
, let
\|\mathbf{x}\|_{p}\coloneqq\left(\sum_{i\in[d]}|x_{i}|^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}
;
\|\mathbf{x}\|_{\infty}\coloneqq\max_{i\in[d]}|x_{i}|=\lim_{p\rightarrow\infty}\|\mathbf{x}\|_{p}
. The transpose of a vector
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is written as
\mathbf{x}^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times n}
. A functional
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{U}}:\mathcal{U}\to[0,\infty)
is referred to as quasi-norm on a vector space
\mathcal{U}
with a constant
K\geq 1
if it satisfies the following 3 properties. (i) Positive definiteness: for all
f\in\mathcal{U}
,
\|f\|_{\mathcal{U}}>0
whenever
f\neq 0
. (ii) Absolute homogeneity: for all
f\in\mathcal{U}
and all
c\in\mathbb{R}
,
\|cf\|_{\mathcal{U}}=|c|\|f\|_{\mathcal{U}}
. (iii) Modified triangle inequality: for all
f,g\in\mathcal{U}
,

\displaystyle\|f+g\|_{\mathcal{U}}\leq K(\|f\|_{\mathcal{U}}+\|g\|_{\mathcal{U}}).

(2)

In other words, the quasi-norm is similar to a norm, but it relaxes the triangle inequality (where one would have
K=1
).
1
1

1

In (
2
),
K
is referred to as the modulus of concavity of
\mathcal{U}
.
A vector space enriched with a quasi-norm is called quasi-normed space, and a quasi-Banach space is a quasi-normed space, where Cauchy sequences are convergent (meant in the sense of the quasi-norm of the space).
2
2

2

For instance, for
0<p\leq 1
the space
\ell^{p}:=\{x=(x_{n})_{n\geq 1}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{Z}^{>0}}:\|x\|_{p}\coloneq\left(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}|x_{n}|^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}<\infty\}

with quasi-norm
\|\cdot\|_{p}
is a quasi-Banach space with constant
K_{p}=2^{\frac{1}{p}-1}
; for
p=1
,
\ell^{p}
is a Banach space.
Let
\mathcal{Z}
be a quasi-Banach space. The ball and sphere in
\mathcal{Z}
with center zero and of radius
r
is defined as
\mathbb{B}_{r}(\mathcal{Z})=\{z\in\mathcal{Z}\,:\,\|z\|_{\mathcal{Z}}\leq r\}
and
\mathbb{S}_{r}(\mathcal{Z})=\{z\in\mathcal{Z}\,:\,\|z\|_{\mathcal{Z}}=r\}
, respectively. We use the shorthand
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\coloneqq\mathbb{S}_{1}\left(\left(\mathbb{R}^{d},\|\cdot\|_{2}\right)\right)
. A function
f:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
is called non-negatively
1
-homogeneous if for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
and for all
a\in\mathbb{R}^{\geq 0}
it holds that
f(ax,ay)=af(x,y)
. Throughout the paper, we assume that our input domain is a compact set
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and it is fixed. Let
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{X})
be the space of continuous real-valued functions on
\mathcal{X}
equipped with the supremum norm
\|f\|_{\infty}\coloneqq\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}}|f(\mathbf{x})|
and the associated Borel sigma-algebra.

A kernel is a symmetric function
k:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
such that for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{>0}
, all
(\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{n})\in\mathcal{X}^{n}
, and all
(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
\sum_{i,j\in[n]}\alpha_{i}k(\mathbf{x}_{i},\mathbf{x}_{j})\alpha_{j}\geq 0
holds. There is a one-to-one correspondence between kernels and reproducing kernel Hilbert spaces (RKHSs), where the latter is a Hilbert space of functions
\mathcal{H}_{k}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{X}}

such that
k_{\mathbf{x}}\coloneqq k(\cdot,\mathbf{x})\in\mathcal{H}_{k}
for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}
and
h(\mathbf{x})=\langle h,k_{\mathbf{x}}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}

for all
(h,\mathbf{x})\in\mathcal{H}_{k}\times\mathcal{X}
; the first property describes the building blocks of the space, the 2nd one is called the reproducing property.
3
3

3

The function
k(\cdot,\mathbf{x})
stands for
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}\mapsto k(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{x})\in\mathbb{R}
while keeping
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}
fixed.
It is known that
\mathcal{H}_{k}=\overline{\Span(k_{\mathbf{x}}\,:\,x\in\mathcal{X})}
, where
\Span(\cdot)
stands for the linear hull of its argument and
\overline{\cdot}
denotes closure. The Brownian kernel is

\displaystyle k^{(\mathrm{B})}(x,x^{\prime})=\frac{|x|+|x^{\prime}|-|x-x^{\prime}|}{2}\quad(x,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R});

(3)

it is non-negatively 1-homogeneous.
4
4

4

Indeed, for any
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
and
a\in\mathbb{R}^{\geq 0}
one has
k^{(\mathrm{B})}(ax,ax^{\prime})=\frac{|ax|+|ax^{\prime}|-|ax-ax^{\prime}|}{2}=|a|\frac{|x|+|x^{\prime}|-|x-x^{\prime}|}{2}=a\frac{|x|+|x^{\prime}|-|x-x^{\prime}|}{2}=ak^{(\mathrm{B})}(x,x^{\prime})
.
Let us given (i) a set
\mathcal{D}
, (ii) an index set
\Omega
which is a topological space enriched with a Borel probability measure
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\Omega)
, (iii) for each
\omega\in\Omega
a kernel
k^{(\omega)}
:
\mathcal{D}\times\mathcal{D}\to\mathbb{R}
with its associated RKHS
\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\omega)}}
, and assume that

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}k^{(\omega)}(x,x)\mathrm{d}\mu(\omega)

\displaystyle<\infty\quad\text{for all}\,\,x\in\mathcal{D}.

(4)

By denoting

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\oplus}

\displaystyle=\left\{\left(f_{\omega}\right)_{\omega\in\Omega}\in\prod_{\omega\in\Omega}\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\omega)}}\,:\,\int_{\Omega}\|f_{\omega}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\omega)}}}^{2}\mathrm{d}\mu(\omega)<\infty\right\},

(5)

enriched with the inner product
\langle f,g\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{\oplus}}=\int_{\Omega}\langle f_{\omega},g_{\omega}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\omega)}}}\mathrm{d}\mu(\omega)
, one can show
(
18
, Theorem 3.1)
that (i)
\mathcal{H}_{\oplus}
is a Hilbert space, (ii)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{k}

\displaystyle=\left\{f:\mathcal{D}\to\mathbb{R}\,:\,f(x)=\int_{\Omega}f_{\omega}(x)\mathrm{d}\mu(\omega)\,\,\forall x\in\mathcal{D},\text{ with }\left(f_{\omega}\right)_{\omega\in\Omega}\in\mathcal{H}_{\oplus}\right\},

(6)

is an RKHS (called integral RKHS) with kernel

k(x,y)=\int_{\Omega}k^{(\omega)}(x,y)\mathrm{d}\mu(\omega)

(x,y\in\mathcal{D})

and (iii) the norm in
\mathcal{H}_{k}
is given by

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\inf_{g\in\mathcal{H}_{\oplus}:f(x)=\int_{\Omega}g_{\omega}(x)\mathrm{d}\mu(\omega),\,\forall x\in\mathcal{D}}\int_{\Omega}\|g_{\omega}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\omega)}}}^{2}\mathrm{d}\mu(\omega).

(7)

)

\mathcal{H}_{\hat{\mu}}^{(l)}

l^{th}
layer of
\mathcal{H}^{(1:L)}_{\hat{\mu}}

(
11
)

\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{(1:L)}

L
-level canonical BKLs

(
13
)

\widehat{C}^{(L)}

L
-complexity

(
14
)

\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{(L)}

L^{th}
layer of
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{(1:L)}

(
15
)

Table 2:
Notations of various functions spaces and complexities.

We introduce in
\Cref
sec:BKL our novel kernel-based hierarchical function class called
L
-level Brownian kernel ladder (
\mathcal{H}^{(1:L)}_{\mu}
) based on integral RKHSs, and leverage them to define
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{(L)}
spaces (
\Cref
sec:complexity-meaures) which we use as hypothesis classes in supervised learning. The analytical and the statistical properties of
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{(L)}
spaces are presented in
\Cref
sec:characterization and
\Cref
sec:stat-analyses, respectively. The definitions of this section (various function spaces and complexities) are summarized in
\Cref
caption:defs for the reader’s convenience.

3.1
Brownian Kernel Ladder

Next we present our proposed kernel-based deep composition of functions. We start with a construction to generate a specific integral RKHS given
(i) a measurable subset
\mathcal{S}
of an RKHS
\mathcal{H}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{X}}
defined
on the compact input domain
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, (ii) a Borel probability measure with finite 1st moment
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{S})
, and (iii) the Brownian kernel
k^{(\mathrm{B})}
[as defined in (
3
)]. The
L
-level application of this procedure (initialized with the linear kernel) will give rise to
L
-level BKL
\mathcal{H}^{(1:L)}_{\mu}
. This is the design we detail next.

Let the domain
\mathcal{X}
, measurable subset
\mathcal{S}
of RKHS
\mathcal{H}
, probability measure
\mu
and Brownian kernel
k^{(\mathrm{B})}
as specified above, and let a function
k\left[\mathcal{S},\mu\right]\colon\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
associated to the pair
(\mathcal{S},\mu)
[in the tuplet
\left(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{S},\mu,k^{(\mathrm{B})}\right)
,
\mathcal{X}
and
k^{(\mathrm{B})}
are kept fixed] be defined as

\displaystyle k\left[\mathcal{S},\mu\right](\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime})=\int_{\mathcal{S}}\underbrace{k^{(\mathrm{B})}\left(u(\mathbf{x}),u\left(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\right)\right)}_{=:k_{u}\left(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\right)}\mathrm{d}\mu(u).

(8)

By the choice
\left(\Omega,\omega,k^{(\omega)}(x,y),\mathcal{D},\mu\right)\leftarrow\left(\mathcal{S},u,k_{u}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}),\mathcal{X},\mu\right)
in the integral kernel construction of Section
2
, one can show (
\Cref
lem:BK) that condition (
4
) reading in our case as

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{S}}k_{u}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x})\mathrm{d}\mu(u)

\displaystyle<\infty,\,\,\text{for all}\,\,\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X},

(9)

holds, hence
k\left[\mathcal{S},\mu\right]
is a well-defined integral kernel. In our paper, we will use this construction with
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{S}=\mathbb{S}_{1}(\mathcal{H})
.

Having specified the kernel
k\left[\mathcal{S},\mu\right]
, we continue with the definition of the proposed
L
-level BKL
\mathcal{H}^{(1:L)}_{\mu}
associated to a sequence of measurable subsets of RKHSs
\mathcal{S}^{(1:L-1)}_{\mu}:=\left(\mathcal{S}_{\mu}^{(l)}\right)_{l=1}^{L-1}
and probability measures
\mu^{(1:L-1)}:=\left(\mu^{(l)}\right)_{l=1}^{L-1}
.

Definition 1

(
\mathcal{H}^{(1:L)}_{\mu}
=
L
-level BKL associated to
\mathcal{S}^{(1:L-1)}_{\mu}
and
\mu^{(1:L-1)}
)
.

Let the level
L\geq 2
be fixed.
Let
\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(1)}
be the RKHS of the linear functionals

\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}\mapsto\bm{\omega}^{\top}\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}
(
\bm{\omega}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
).
For each
l\in[L-1]
, consider a measurable subset
\mathcal{S}_{\mu}^{(l)}\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(l)}
and
let
\mu^{(l)}\in\mathcal{P}_{1}\left(\mathcal{S}_{\mu}^{(l)}\right)
be a probability measure on
\mathcal{S}_{\mu}^{(l)}
with finite first moment.
Form the sequence of integral RKHSs defined recursively as

\displaystyle k^{(l)}\coloneqq k\left[\mathcal{S}_{\mu}^{(l)},\mu^{(l)}\right]\quad\text{defined according to }\eqref{eq:BK_def},\qquad\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(l+1)}\coloneqq\mathcal{H}_{k^{(l)}},\quad l\in[L-1].

We refer to the resulting sequence

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{(1:L)}_{\mu}\coloneqq\left(\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(l)}\right)_{l=1}^{L}

as the
L
-level Brownian kernel ladder (BKL) associated to
\mathcal{S}^{(1:L-1)}_{\mu}
and
\mu^{(1:L-1)}
,
with elements
f^{(1:L)}\coloneqq\left(f^{(l)}\right)_{l=1}^{L}\in\mathcal{H}^{(1:L)}_{\mu}
,
and we call
\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(l)}
as the
l^{th}
layer of
\mathcal{H}^{(1:L)}_{\mu}
.
5
5

5

Notice that

\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(l)}
depends on
\mathcal{S}^{(1:l-1)}_{\mu}\coloneq\left(\mathcal{S}_{\mu}^{(i)}\right)_{i=1}^{l-1}
and
\mu^{(1:l-1)}\coloneq\left(\mu^{(i)}\right)_{i=1}^{l-1}
; we use the shorthand
\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(l)}:=\mathcal{H}^{(l)}_{\mathcal{S}^{(1:l-1)}_{\mu},\mu^{(1:l-1)}}
to keep the notation light.

{assumption}

[normalization]
For all
l\in[L-1]
,
\mathcal{S}_{\mu}^{(l)}=\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(l)}
and
\mu^{(l)}\in\mathcal{P}_{1,1}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(l)}\right)
.

Remark 2
.

1.

Design of
\mathcal{H}^{(1:L)}_{\mu}
:
The definition of BKL can be interpreted as follows. Starting at the first level with linear functionals (
\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(1)}
), at each level
l\in[L-1]
one selects a measurable subset
\mathcal{S}_{\mu}^{(l)}\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(l)}
and a probability measure on
\mathcal{S}_{\mu}^{(l)}
with finite first moment
\mu^{(l)}\in\mathcal{P}_{1}\left(\mathcal{S}_{\mu}^{(l)}\right)
. This subset-measure pair
\left(\mathcal{S}_{\mu}^{(l)},\mu^{(l)}\right)
is used to construct the integral kernel
k^{(l)}
via (
8
) by applying the substitution

\displaystyle(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{S},\mu)\ \leftarrow\ \left(\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(l)},\,\mathcal{S}_{\mu}^{(l)},\,\mu^{(l)}\right),

which determines the next-level integral RKHS
\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(l+1)}=\mathcal{H}_{k^{(l)}}
.

2.

Choice of
\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(1)}
: The choice of
\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(1)}
to be the RKHS of linear functionals, is used in the verification of (
9
) (
\Cref
lem:BK).

3.

Alternative representation of the elements
f^{(1:L)}\in\mathcal{H}^{(1:L)}_{\mu}
: One can show (
\Cref
coro:IRKHS_BKL) that the elements
f^{(1:L)}=\left(f^{(l)}\right)_{l=1}^{L}\in\mathcal{H}^{(1:L)}_{\mu}
take the form

\displaystyle f^{(l+1)}(\mathbf{x})=\int_{\mathcal{S}_{\mu}^{(l)}}g_{u}^{(l)}\left(u(\mathbf{x})\right)\mathrm{d}\mu^{(l)}(u),\quad\forall\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X},l\in[L-1],

where
g_{u}^{(l)}\in\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}
for all
u\in\mathcal{S}_{\mu}^{(l)}
.

4.

Canonical form of BKL in case of
\Cref
assumption:normalizing: Supposing
\Cref
assumption:normalizing, one can show (Lemma
17
) that there exists a probability measure on the unit sphere of
\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(l)}
with first moment equal to one

\hat{\mu}^{(l)}\in\mathcal{P}_{1,1}\left(\mathbb{S}_{1}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(l)}\right)\right)
for
l\in[L-1]

such that for the associated integral kernel

\displaystyle\hat{k}^{(l)}=k\left[\mathbb{S}_{1}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(l)}\right),\,\hat{\mu}^{(l)}\right],

(10)

one has
\hat{k}^{(l)}=k^{(l)}
for
l\in[L-1]
. Specifically this means that

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{(1:L)}_{\mu}=\mathcal{H}^{(1:L)}_{\hat{\mu}}\eqcolon\left(\mathcal{H}_{\hat{\mu}}^{(l)}\right)_{l=1}^{L},

(11)

where
\mathcal{H}_{\hat{\mu}}^{(l+1)}=\mathcal{H}_{\hat{k}^{(l)}}
for
l\in[L-1]
and
\mathcal{H}_{\hat{\mu}}^{(1)}\coloneq\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(1)}
. In other words, under
\Cref
assumption:normalizing one can assume w.l.o.g. that
\mathcal{S}_{\mu}^{(l)}=\mathbb{S}_{1}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(l)}\right)
for
l\in[L-1]
, while preserving the associated kernels and integral RKHSs in BKL.

5.

Similarities of the underlying kernel of BKLs, Brownian kernel neural networks (BKERNN;
13
) and neural Hilbert ladders (NHLs;
9
): In the specific case of
L=2
, identifying
\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(1)}
with
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and choosing
\mathcal{S}_{\mu}^{(1)}:=\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, the integral kernel in the BKL construction [see (
8
)] takes the form

\displaystyle k\left[\mathbb{S}^{d-1},\hat{\mu}\right]\left(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\right)=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}k^{(\mathrm{B})}\left(\bm{\omega}^{\top}\mathbf{x},\bm{\omega}^{\top}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\hat{\mu}\left(\bm{\omega}\right),\,\,\hat{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{1,1}\left(\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\right);

this is the same kernel as the one used in BKERNNs.
L
-level (
L\geq 2
) NHLs and BKLs share a similar hierarchical construction, with the former applying the integral kernel

\displaystyle k\left[\mathcal{H},\mu\right](\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{H}}\sigma(u(\mathbf{x}))\sigma(u(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}))\mathrm{d}\mu(u),

(12)

instead of (
8
), with
\sigma
representing a Lipschitz activation function.
Two important differences of our associated hypothesis class
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{(L)}
(defined at the end of
\Cref
sec:complexity-meaures): (i) in supervised learning
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{(L)}
has significantly improved generalization property compared to BKERNNs (detailed after
\Cref
thm:excess-risk-BKL, in
\Cref
sec:excess-risk-guarantees), (ii)
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{(L)}
gives bounded dependence on the number of layers (
L
) in contrast to NHLs which have a
\sqrt{L}
dependence (elaborated in bullet
3
of
\Cref
rem:Gaussian_complexity_thm).

Using the hierarchical structure of
\mathcal{H}^{(1:L)}_{\mu}
, we define a complexity measure which will be used to provide Gaussian complexity bound (Theorem
8
) paving the way to generalization analysis (in
\Cref
sec:excess-risk-guarantees); the former is what we detail next.

3.2
BKL Spaces and Complexity Measures

In this section, we introduce a new function class
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{(L)}
[relying on
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{(1:L)}
which will be detailed in (
13
) and is based on
\mathcal{H}^{(1:L)}_{\hat{\mu}}
defined in (
11
)], and a complexity measure associated to its top layer. This complexity measure will allow us to obtain dimension- and depth-independent generalization guarantees while using
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{(L)}
as hypothesis class in supervised learning (Section
4
). The definitions presented below follow the order in
\Cref
caption:defs, while noting that we already covered the first
4
lines of the table.

Let
\mathcal{X}
,
L
and
d
be fixed. Let
\Cref
assumption:normalizing hold. Recall that in this case by bullet
4
in
\Cref
rem:BKL_def, we (can) consider w.l.o.g. the canonical version of BKL and assume that
\mathcal{S}^{(1:L-1)}_{\hat{\mu}}\coloneq\left(\mathbb{S}_{1}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(l)}\right)\right)_{l=1}^{L-1}
and
\hat{\mu}^{(1:L-1)}\in\times_{l=1}^{L-1}\mathcal{P}_{1,1}\left(\mathbb{S}_{1}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(l)}\right)\right)
. Varying the probability measures
\hat{\mu}^{(1:L-1)}
gives rise to the family of functions

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{(1:L)}=\bigcup_{\hat{\mu}^{(1:L-1)}\in\times_{l=1}^{L-1}\mathcal{P}_{1,1}\left(\mathbb{S}_{1}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(l)}\right)\right)}\mathcal{H}^{(1:L)}_{\hat{\mu}},

(13)

which we will refer to as
L
-level canonical BKL.

Complexity of the top layer elements of
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{(1:L)}
.

Assume now that
f
lies in the top layer of some
\mathcal{H}^{(1:L)}_{\hat{\mu}}
, that is,
f\in\mathcal{H}_{\hat{\mu}}^{(L)}
for some
\mathcal{H}^{(1:L)}_{\hat{\mu}}\in\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{(1:L)}
. We define the
L
-complexity of
f
as the optimum of all such realizations:

\displaystyle\widehat{C}^{(L)}(f)\coloneqq\inf_{\mathcal{H}^{(1:L)}_{\hat{\mu}}\in\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{(1:L)}\,:\,f\in\mathcal{H}_{\hat{\mu}}^{(L)}}\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\hat{\mu}}^{(L)}}\in[0,\infty).

(14)

Space
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{(L)}
.

Finally, we define the space
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{(L)}
as the collection of
L^{th}
-layers of canonical BKLs
\mathcal{H}^{(1:L)}_{\hat{\mu}}
as follows

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{(L)}\coloneqq\left\{f\in\mathcal{H}_{\hat{\mu}}^{(L)}:\mathcal{H}^{(1:L)}_{\hat{\mu}}\in\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{(1:L)},\,\,\widehat{C}^{(L)}(f)<\infty\right\}=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{H}^{(1:L)}_{\hat{\mu}}\in\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{(1:L)}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{H}_{\hat{\mu}}^{(L)}.

(15)

We continue with our results on various properties of the space
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{(L)}
.

4
Results: Analytical and Statistical Properties of

\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{(L)}

This section is dedicated to our results on the analytical (
\Cref
sec:characterization) and statistical properties (
\Cref
sec:stat-analyses) of the function class
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{(L)}
introduced in (
15
).

4.1
Analytical Properties of
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{(L)}

In this subsection, we establish different analytical properties of
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{(L)}
.

Theorem 3

(analytical properties of
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{(L)}
)
.

Let Assumption
3.1
hold. Let
L\geq 2
. Then, the followings hold.

1.

Hölder property, pointwise evaluation: Each element
f\in\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{(L)}
is
\frac{1}{2^{L-1}}
-Hölder continuous with constant
\widehat{C}^{(L)}(f)
:

\displaystyle\left|f\left(\mathbf{x}\right)-f\left(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt[2^{L-1}]{\|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\|_{2}}\widehat{C}^{(L)}(f)\text{ for all }\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}.

(16)

Moreover, a similar property holds for pointwise evaluation

\displaystyle|f(\mathbf{x})|\leq\sqrt[2^{L-1}]{\|\mathbf{x}\|_{2}}\widehat{C}^{(L)}(f),\quad\forall\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}.

(17)

2.

Quasi-Banach structure: The pair
\left(\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{(L)},\widehat{C}^{(L)}\right)
forms a quasi-Banach space.

3.

(Strict) monotonicity:
Let
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{(L)}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{(L+1)}
be defined according to
(
15
).
Then, for every
L\geq 2
, one has
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{(L)}\subseteq\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{(L+1)}
.
Assume that there exist
\mathbf{x}_{0}\in\mathcal{X}
,
\rho>0
,
constants
0<c_{1}\leq C_{1}<\infty
, a Lipschitz map

\gamma:[0,\rho]\to\mathcal{X}
with
\gamma(0)=\mathbf{x}_{0}
, and a function

u_{1}\in\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(1)}
such that

\displaystyle u_{1}(\mathbf{x}_{0})=0,\qquad c_{1}t\leq u_{1}(\gamma(t))\leq C_{1}t,\qquad t\in[0,\rho].

Then there exists a function
f_{\star}\in\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{(L+1)}\setminus\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{(L)}

such that for every
r>0
,

\displaystyle\inf_{g\in\mathcal{B}^{(L)}_{r}}\left\|f_{\star}-g\right\|_{\infty}>0,\qquad\mathcal{B}^{(L)}_{r}\coloneqq\mathbb{B}_{r}\left(\left(\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{(L)},\widehat{C}^{(L)}\right)\right).

(18)

Specifically, this means that for every
L\geq 2
,
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{(L)}\subsetneq\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{(L+1)}
.

Remark 4
.

1.

Metric on
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{(L)}
[
\Cref
thm:charachteriz_funspace
2
]: The space
\left(\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{(L)},\widehat{C}^{(L)}\right)
is a quasi-norm space, hence in particular it is a linear space
equipped with a quasi-norm
\widehat{C}^{(L)}
satisfying

\displaystyle\widehat{C}^{(L)}(f+g)\leq K\left[\widehat{C}^{(L)}(f)+\widehat{C}^{(L)}(g)\right],\qquad f,g\in\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{(L)},

(19)

with
K=K(L)=\frac{2}{\sqrt[2^{L}]{2}}\in\left[2^{\frac{3}{4}},2\right)
where the value of
K
is shown in the proof of
\Cref
thm:charachteriz_funspace
2
.
6
6

6

The function
L\in\{2,3,4,\ldots\}\mapsto K(L)\in\mathbb{R}
is non-decreasing and converging to 2 as
L\to\infty
.
Therefore, by the Aoki-Rolewicz theorem
(
20
, Theorem
1.3
)

there exists
\alpha=\alpha(K)\in(0,1]
and a quasi-norm
\left(\widehat{C}^{(L)}\right)^{*}
on
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{(L)}
equivalent to
\widehat{C}^{(L)}
for which the metric

\displaystyle d^{(L)}(f,g)\coloneqq\left[\left(\widehat{C}^{(L)}\right)^{*}(f-g)\right]^{\alpha},\qquad f,g\in\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{(L)}

induces the same topology as the quasi-norm
\widehat{C}^{(L)}
. This metric allows one to work with (classical) Cauchy sequences.

2.

Expressivity of
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{(L)}
[
\Cref
thm:charachteriz_funspace
3
]: The expressivity of BKL increases as a function of the depth
L
. (
18
) shows that one can find some element
f_{*}
in
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{(L+1)}
but not in
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{(L)}
, whose distance from arbitrary ball of
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{(L)}
is positive.

3.

Assumptions of strict increase [
\Cref
thm:charachteriz_funspace
3
]: These assumptions are mild. Indeed, they hold (
\Cref
lem:line-segment-implies-trace), for instance, if
\mathcal{X}
contains a non-degenerate line segment, that is, when there exist
\mathbf{v}_{1},\mathbf{v}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\mathbf{v}_{1}\neq\mathbf{v}_{2}
such that

\displaystyle\left\{(1-t)\mathbf{v}_{1}+t\mathbf{v}_{2}:t\in[0,1]\right\}\subseteq\mathcal{X}.

4.2
Statistical Properties of
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{(L)}

In this section we prove excess risk guarantees for the proposed function class
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{(L)}
. Let us recall the key idea of supervised learning. One is given a training set
\mathcal{D}_{XY,n}=\{(x_{i},y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}\sim\nu_{XY}^{n}
, where (i) the joint distribution describing the relation of
X
and
Y
is
\nu_{XY}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y})
, (ii)
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}\subset\mathbb{R}
is referred to as the input space and the output space, respectively, (iii) the distribution of the input
X
is denoted by
\nu_{X}
.
7
7

7

The notation
\nu_{X}
will be used when discussing Gaussian complexities (
\Cref
sec:gauss-complex).
The goal is to learn a function
f
based on the data
\mathcal{D}_{XY,n}
such that
f(x)
is a ’good’ approximation of the output
y\in\mathcal{Y}
when a new input
x\in\mathcal{X}
is presented. For a candidate function
f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
and an input-output pair
(x,y)\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}
the quality of the approximation is measured via a loss function
\ell:\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{Y}\to\mathbb{R}
as
\ell(f(x),y)
; the performance of
f
is quantified via its average loss

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(f)\coloneqq\int_{\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}}\ell(f(x),y)\mathrm{d}\nu_{XY}(x,y)

(20)

called expected risk. In practice, one typically optimizes a proxy for this problem

\displaystyle\hat{f}_{\lambda}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\argmin_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\mathcal{R}_{n}(f)+\lambda\mathit{C}(f),\quad\lambda>0

(21)

referred to as regularized empirical risk minimization (RERM) with (i) the empirical risk

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{n}(f)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\int_{\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}}\ell(f(x),y)\mathrm{d}\nu_{XY,n}(x,y)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in[n]}\ell(f(x_{i}),y_{i})

(22)

associated to the empirical measure
\nu_{XY,n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{(x_{i},y_{i})}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y})
arising from the training data
\mathcal{D}_{XY,n}
, (ii) hypothesis class
\mathcal{F}
and regularization
\mathit{C}
(such as a function of the underlying norm in
\mathcal{F}
), (iii)
\lambda
capturing the tradeoff between the two terms. The aim of learning is to minimize the performance difference compared to the optimal risk
as quantified by the excess risk

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(f)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}(f)-\inf_{g\in\mathcal{F}}\mathcal{R}(g);

(23)

the excess risk is non-negative by definition, and it is equal to zero for any minimizer (
f^{*}\in\argmin_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\mathcal{R}(f)
; if it exists). We choose a compact input space
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the hypothesis class to be
\mathcal{F}=\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{(L)}
, with regularization
C=\widehat{C}^{(L)}
, and hence work with

\displaystyle\hat{f}_{\lambda}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{f\in\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{(L)}}J_{n}(f),

\displaystyle J_{n}(f)

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{R}_{n}(f)+\lambda\widehat{C}^{(L)}(f),\quad\lambda>0,

(24)

\displaystyle f^{*}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{f\in\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{(L)}}\mathcal{R}(f).

(25)

After establishing in
\Cref
sec:RERM-solution the existence of the RERM solution(s)
\hat{f}_{\lambda}
in (
24
) and the uniqueness of
f^{*}
in (
25
), we derive excess risk guarantees
\mathcal{E}\left(\hat{f}_{\lambda}\right)
for (
24
) in
\Cref
sec:excess-risk-guarantees via proving bounds in
\Cref
sec:gauss-complex for the Gaussian complexity
(
5
)
of the
r
-
ball of
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{(L)}
. This is what we elaborate in the following.

4.2.1
Existence of the Solution

In the following result, we establish the existence of
\hat{f}_{\lambda}
[defined in (
24
)] and the uniqueness
f^{*}
[defined in (
25
)].

Theorem 5

(existence of
\hat{f}_{\lambda}
, uniqueness of
f^{*}
)
.

Let Assumption
3.1
hold. Let
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be compact and
\mathcal{Y}\subset\mathbb{R}
.

1.

Existence of
\hat{f}_{\lambda}
: Let
\mathcal{D}_{XY,n}=\{(\mathbf{x}_{i},y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}\subset\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}
be a fixed sample. Let the RERM objective (
24
) be associated to a loss function
\ell:\mathcal{Y}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
and the data
\mathcal{D}_{XY,n}
. Assume that
\ell(y,\cdot)
is continuous for all
y\in\mathcal{Y}
. Then, there exists a minimizer
\hat{f}_{\lambda}\in\argmin_{f\in\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{(L)}}J_{n}(f)
.

2.

Uniqueness of
f^{*}
(if it exists):
Assume that the conditions of the previous item hold. Suppose also that for all
y\in\mathcal{Y}
, the function
\ell(y,\cdot):\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
is strictly convex.
8
8

8

In other words, for all
u_{1}\neq u_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
and all
t\in(0,1)
,

\ell\big(y,tu_{1}+(1-t)u_{2}\big)<t\,\ell(y,u_{1})+(1-t)\,\ell(y,u_{2})
.

Then any minimizer
f^{*}
defined in (
25
), if it exists, is unique.

Remark 6
.

Since
\widehat{C}^{(L)}
is a quasi-norm [see (
19
)], the solution of (
24
) (
\hat{f}_{\lambda}
) might not be unique.

4.2.2
Gaussian Complexity

To our statistical analysis (
\Cref
sec:excess-risk-guarantees) in this section we bound the Gaussian complexity
\mathcal{G}_{n}\left(\mathcal{F}\right)
with
\mathcal{F}\leftarrow\mathcal{B}^{(L)}_{r}
[the latter defined in (
18
)] chosen to be the ball with center zero and radius
r
of
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{(L)}
.

Definition 7

((empirical) Gaussian complexity)
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a real-valued function class over
\mathcal{X}
and
\bm{\varepsilon}=(\varepsilon_{i})_{i=1}^{n}\sim\mathcal{N}^{n}(0,1)
. Then for a fixed dataset
\mathcal{D}_{n}=\{\mathbf{x}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}\subset\mathcal{X}
the empirical Gaussian complexity of
\mathcal{F}
is defined as

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{n}(\mathcal{F})\coloneqq\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{N}^{n}(0,1)}\left[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}f(\mathbf{x}_{i})\right],

(26)

and its expectation w.r.t.
(X_{i})_{i=1}^{n}\sim\nu_{X}^{n}

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{n}(\mathcal{F})\coloneqq\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{X}^{n}}\left[\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{n}(\mathcal{F})\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{N}^{n}(0,1),\nu_{X}^{n}}\left[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}f(X_{i})\right],

(27)

is called the Gaussian complexity of
\mathcal{F}
.

Our next result shows that
\mathcal{G}_{n}\left(\mathcal{B}^{(L)}_{r}\right)
, with
\mathcal{B}^{(L)}_{r}
defined in (
18
), is independent of the dimension
d
.

Theorem 8

(Gaussian complexity of
\mathcal{B}^{(L)}_{r}
)
.

Let Assumption
3.1
hold. Then there exists a universal constant
C>0
such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{n}\left(\mathcal{B}^{(L)}_{r}\right)\leq\frac{Cr\ln(2n)}{\sqrt{n}}\,\sqrt[2^{L}]{\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{X}^{n}}\left[\max_{i\in[n]}\|X_{i}\|_{2}^{2}\right]}.

(28)

Remark 9
.

1.

Uniform control w.r.t.
L
, choice of
k^{(\mathrm{B})}
: Let
9
9

9

The compactness of
\mathcal{X}
implies the finiteness of
B
.

\displaystyle B:=\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}}\left\|\mathbf{x}\right\|_{2}^{2}<\infty.

(29)

Then, in (
28
)

\displaystyle a_{L}

\displaystyle\eqcolon\sqrt[2^{L}]{\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{X}^{n}}\left[\max_{i\in[n]}\|X_{i}\|_{2}^{2}\right]}\leq\sqrt[2^{L}]{B}.

Noticing that the function
L\in\{2,3,\ldots\}\mapsto\sqrt[2^{L}]{B}\in\mathbb{R}
is non-increasing,
a_{L}\leq\sqrt[4]{B}
for any
L\geq 2
. Hence, the Gaussian complexity can be upper bounded
uniformly over
L
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{n}\left(\mathcal{B}^{(L)}_{r}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{Cr\ln(2n)\sqrt[4]{B}}{\sqrt{n}}.

(30)

This bounded behavior of
a_{L}
as a function of
L
relies on the non-negative 1-homogeneous property of the Brownian kernel and the Hölder property [discussed in
\Cref
thm:charachteriz_funspace
1
].

2.

Difference compared to BKERNN
(
13
)
: As elaborated in item
5
of Remark
2
, the BKL construction when specialized to
L=2
uses the same kernel as BKERNNs.
For this setting, a dimension-independent Gaussian complexity bound
has recently been established by
13
,
namely

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{n}\left(\mathcal{B}^{(2)}_{1}\right)\leq\frac{1}{n^{1/6}}\left(\mathbb{E}_{X^{n}}\left[\max_{i\in[n]}\|X_{i}\|_{2}^{2}\right]\right)^{1/4}.

(31)

In contrast, our Gaussian complexity bound of order
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(n^{-1/2}\right)
in
(
30
) holds
uniformly for all depths
L\geq 2
,
and significantly improves upon the

\mathcal{O}\left(n^{-1/6}\right)
rate in (
31
) even in the
shallow case of
L=2
.

3.

Difference compared to NHLs
(
9
)
: Instead of Gaussian complexity as in (
27
),
9
showed a Rademacher complexity bound of order
\mathcal{O}\left(\sqrt{L}n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\right)
on the
L
-level NHL architecture. In contrast, our Gaussian complexity result gives a
bounded dependence on the number of layers
(
L
).

4.2.3
Excess Risk Analysis

In this section we present generalization guarantees for learning with BKLs.
Building upon the Gaussian complexity bound obtained in
\Cref
thm:peeling_all_depth_BKL, we prove that the RERM solution with BKL taken as the hypothesis class enjoys nonparametric rate
\mathcal{O}\left(n^{-1/2}\right)
(up to logarithmic factor) for the excess risk. Importantly, the depth level
L
does not alter the statistical rate. This result shows that the rich representational capacity of BKLs also comes with strong statistical guarantees.

We impose a well-specified assumption; this is common in the non-parametric regression literature
(
8
)
.

{assumption}

[well-specified case]
There exists
f^{*}\in\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{(L)}
such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}\left(f^{*}\right)=\inf_{f\in\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{(L)}}\mathcal{R}(f).

Having introduced this well-specified assumption, our key result on the generalization properties of BKLs is as follows.

Theorem 10

(Excess risk bound for RERM over BKLs)
.

Let Assumptions
3.1
-
4.2.3
hold. Let
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be compact with
B
defined in (
29
). Let
\mathcal{Y}=[-M,M]
for some
M\geq 0
. Let the number of levels of BKL be
L\geq 2
. Let
\ell:\mathcal{Y}\times\mathbb{R}\to[0,M_{\ell}]

be
L_{\ell}
-Lipschitz in its 2nd argument:

\displaystyle\forall y\in\mathcal{Y},\forall u_{1},u_{2}\in\mathbb{R}:\quad|\ell(y,u_{1})-\ell(y,u_{2})|\leq L_{\ell}|u_{1}-u_{2}|.

(32)

Let the excess risk be
\mathcal{E}
[as defined in (
23
)]. Let
\lambda=\frac{16\sqrt{\pi}L_{\ell}C\ln(2n)\sqrt[4]{B}}{\sqrt{2n}}+16L_{\ell}\sqrt[4]{B}\sqrt{\frac{2\ln\left(1/\delta\right)}{n}}
. Let
\hat{f}_{\lambda}
be any solution of (
24
). Then, there exists a universal constant
C>0
such that for all
\delta\in(0,1)
, with probability at least
1-\delta
, one has

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}\left(\hat{f}_{\lambda}\right)\leq\frac{16\widehat{C}^{(L)}\left(f^{*}\right)L_{\ell}\sqrt[4]{B}}{\sqrt{n}}\left[\frac{\sqrt{\pi}C\ln(2n)}{\sqrt{2}}+\sqrt{\ln(1/\delta)}\right].

(33)

Remark 11
.

1.

Statistical rate, dimension-free generalization: The established excess risk guarantee in (
33
) (i) matches the classical
\mathcal{O}\left(n^{-1/2}\right)
rate achieved in RKHSs
(
11
;
7
;
27
;
7
)
up to logarithmic factors, (ii) is
independent
of the ambient dimension
d
, indicating that BKLs can circumvent the curse of dimensionality in terms of generalization guarantees.

2.

Comparison to BKERNNs
(
13
)
: The Gaussian complexity bound derived for BKERNN with
L=2
yields an excess risk guarantee of the form
\mathcal{E}\left(\hat{f}_{\lambda}\right)=\mathcal{O}\left(n^{-1/6}\right)

(
13
, Theorem 9)
. In contrast, our Gaussian complexity analysis for BKLs leads to the significantly sharper excess risk bound
\mathcal{E}\left(\hat{f}_{\lambda}\right)=\mathcal{\tilde{O}}\left(n^{-1/2}\right)
, which holds
uniformly for all depths

L\geq 2
.

5
Conclusion

In this work, we introduced Brownian kernel ladders (BKLs), a hierarchy of recursively generated integral reproducing kernel Hilbert spaces constructed through Brownian-kernel integral representations. The proposed framework provides a function-space model in which hierarchical structure is encoded directly through recursive integral RKHS constructions. Based on this framework, we defined canonical BKL spaces together with an associated complexity functional and established several analytical and statistical properties of the resulting hierarchy.

From the analytical perspective, we proved that the associated function classes form quasi-Banach spaces, satisfy depth-dependent Hölder regularity estimates, and exhibit strict monotonicity of the associated function-space hierarchy with respect to the depth parameter. From the statistical perspective, we established Gaussian complexity bounds for balls in BKL spaces that remain uniformly controlled with respect to both the ambient dimension and the depth parameter, together with excess-risk guarantees of order
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(n^{-1/2}\right)
for regularized empirical risk minimization over BKL spaces. A key technical component of the analysis was a new combinatorial proof technique based on recursive subset decompositions and threshold representations associated with the Brownian kernel.

The present work opens several directions for future research. On the approximation-theoretic side, it would be interesting to investigate universality properties, approximation rates, and depth-dependent representation phenomena within the BKL hierarchy. In particular, understanding how recursive Brownian-kernel constructions influence approximation efficiency and compositional representations remains an important open question.

Another natural direction concerns optimization and computational aspects of learning with BKLs. While the present work develops the functional-analytic and statistical foundations of the framework, studying variational formulations, finite-dimensional approximations, sparse representations, and scalable optimization procedures for BKL models remains an important topic for future investigation.
