\displaystyle=\left\{b^{(l)}=\left(b_{u}^{(l)}\right)_{u\in\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(l)}}\in\prod_{u\in\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(l)}}\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}\,:\,\int_{\mathcal{S}_{\mu}^{(l)}}\left\|b_{u}^{(l)}\right\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}}^{2}\mathrm{d}\mu^{(l)}(u)<\infty\right\}.

Proof.

One gets that for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}

\displaystyle f^{(l+1)}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\int_{\mathcal{S}_{\mu}^{(l)}}h_{u}^{(l)}(\mathbf{x})\mathrm{d}\mu^{(l)}(u)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{=}}\int_{\mathcal{S}_{\mu}^{(l)}}g_{u}^{(l)}\left(u(\mathbf{x})\right)\mathrm{d}\mu^{(l)}(u),\text{ with}

\displaystyle\left(h_{u}^{(l)}\right)_{u\in\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(l)}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\in}}\mathcal{H}_{\oplus}^{(l)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}\left\{a^{(l)}=\left(a_{u}^{(l)}\right)_{u\in\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(l)}}\in\prod_{u\in\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(l)}}\mathcal{H}_{k_{u}}\,:\,\int_{\mathcal{S}_{\mu}^{(l)}}\left\|a_{u}^{(l)}\right\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k_{u}}}^{2}\mathrm{d}\mu^{(l)}(u)<\infty\right\},

\displaystyle\left(g_{u}^{(l)}\right)_{u\in\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(l)}}

\displaystyle\in\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{\oplus}^{(l)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(e)}}{{=}}\left\{b^{(l)}=\left(b_{u}^{(l)}\right)_{u\in\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(l)}}\in\prod_{u\in\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(l)}}\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}\,:\,\int_{\mathcal{S}_{\mu}^{(l)}}\left\|b_{u}^{(l)}\right\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}}^{2}\mathrm{d}\mu^{(l)}(u)<\infty\right\},

\displaystyle\left\|f^{(l+1)}\right\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(l+1)}}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\hskip-66.86414pt\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{=}}\inf\left\{\int_{\mathcal{S}_{\mu}^{(l)}}\left\|a_{u}^{(l)}\right\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k_{u}}}^{2}\mathrm{d}\mu^{(l)}(u)\,:\,f^{(l+1)}(\mathbf{x})=\int_{\mathcal{S}_{\mu}^{(l)}}a_{u}^{(l)}(\mathbf{x})\mathrm{d}\mu^{(l)}(u),\,\forall\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X},a^{(l)}\in\mathcal{H}_{\oplus}^{(l)}\right\}

\displaystyle\hskip-66.86414pt\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(f)}}{{=}}\inf\left\{\int_{\mathcal{S}_{\mu}^{(l)}}\left\|b_{u}^{(l)}\right\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}}^{2}\mathrm{d}\mu^{(l)}(u)\,:\,f^{(l+1)}(\mathbf{x})=\int_{\mathcal{S}_{\mu}^{(l)}}b_{u}^{(l)}(u(\mathbf{x}))\mathrm{d}\mu^{(l)}(u),\,\forall\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X},b^{(l)}\in\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{\oplus}^{(l)}\right\}.

(a) follows from (
6
) with
h_{u}^{(l)}\in\mathcal{H}_{k_{u}}
, (b) comes from (
5
), (c) is implied by (
7
). For
h_{u}^{(l)}\in\mathcal{H}_{k_{u}}
,
\Cref
lem:kernel_transformation with a substitution
(k,\mathcal{Y})\leftarrow(k^{(\mathrm{B})},\mathbb{R})
guarantees the existence of
g_{u}^{(l)}\in\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}
such that
h_{u}^{(l)}=g_{u}^{(l)}\circ u
, this gives (d). With the same substitution,
\Cref
lem:kernel_transformation implies (e) and (f).
∎

Lemma 16

(kernel transformation)
.

Let
u:\mathcal{X}\to\operatorname{Im}(u)=:\mathcal{Z}
be a function,
k:\mathcal{Z}\times\mathcal{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
a kernel, and let us define
k_{u}(x,x^{\prime})\coloneqq k(u(x),u(x^{\prime}))
(
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}
) the
u
-altered kernel. Then the followings hold.

1.

For any
h\in\mathcal{H}_{k_{u}}
, there exists
g\in\mathcal{H}_{k}
such that
h=g\circ u
(in short,
\mathcal{H}_{k_{u}}\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{k}\circ u:=\{g\circ u\,:\,g\in\mathcal{H}_{k}\}
and
\|h\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k_{u}}}=\|g\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}
, and

2.

For
g\in\mathcal{H}_{k}
,
h:=g\circ u\in\mathcal{H}_{k_{u}}
(in short,
\mathcal{H}_{k}\circ u\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{k_{u}}
) and
\|h\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k_{u}}}=\|g\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}
.

3.

Let
k_{u}(x,x^{\prime})\coloneqq k^{(\mathrm{B})}(u(x),u(x^{\prime}))
(
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}
) with
k^{(\mathrm{B})}
denoting the Brownian kernel. Then, we have
\alpha k_{u}(\cdot,x)=k_{\alpha u}(\cdot,x)
for all
\alpha\geq 0
and all
x\in\mathcal{X}
.

Proof.

•

Part-1
(
\mathcal{H}_{k_{u}}\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{k}\circ u
direction):
Consider the pre-RKHS

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{k_{u},0}

\displaystyle:=\Span(k_{u}(\cdot,x)\,:\,x\in\mathcal{X})

\displaystyle=\left\{\sum_{i\in[n]}\alpha_{i}k_{u}(\cdot,x_{i})\,:\,n\in\mathbb{Z}^{>0},(x_{i})_{i=1}^{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{n},(\alpha_{i})_{i=1}^{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\right\},

and let
h=\sum_{i\in[n]}\alpha_{i}k_{u}(\cdot,x_{i})\in\mathcal{H}_{k_{u},0}
.

–

Existence of
g\in\mathcal{H}_{k}
s.t.
h=g\circ u
: Taking
g\in\mathcal{H}_{k}
defined as
g:z\in\mathcal{Z}\mapsto\sum_{i\in[d]}\alpha_{i}k(z,u(x_{i}))\in\mathbb{R}
, one gets that
h=g\circ u
holds since

\displaystyle h(x)=\sum_{i\in[n]}\alpha_{i}k_{u}(x,x_{i})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\sum_{i\in[n]}\alpha_{i}k(u(x),u(x_{i}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}g(u(x)),\quad x\in\mathcal{X},

where (a) and (b) follow from the definition of
k_{u}
and that of
g
, respectively.

–

\|h\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k_{u}}}=\|g\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}
: Continuing the reasoning of the previous point,

\displaystyle\|h\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k_{u}}}^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\left\langle\sum_{i\in[n]}\alpha_{i}k_{u}(\cdot,x_{i}),\sum_{j\in[n]}\alpha_{j}k_{u}(\cdot,x_{j})\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k_{u}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}\sum_{i,j\in[n]}\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}\left\langle k_{u}(\cdot,x_{i}),k_{u}(\cdot,x_{j})\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k_{u}}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{=}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}k_{u}(x_{i},x_{j})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{=}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}k(u(x_{i}),u(x_{j}))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{=}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}\langle k(\cdot,u(x_{i})),k(\cdot,u(x_{j}))\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}\left\langle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}k(\cdot,u(x_{i})),\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}k(\cdot,u(x_{j}))\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(e)}}{{=}}\langle g,g\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\|g\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}^{2}.

In (a) we used that in a Hilbert space the norm is induced by the inner product, (b) follows from the linearity of the inner product, (c) is implied by the reproducing property, (d) comes from the definition of
k_{u}
, in (e) the definition of
g
was applied.

The results extend to
\mathcal{H}_{k_{u}}
by the denseness of
\mathcal{H}_{k_{u},0}
in
\mathcal{H}_{k_{u}}
.

•

Part-2
(
\mathcal{H}_{k}\circ u\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{k_{u}}
direction): Let

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{k,0}:=\Span(k(\cdot,z)\,:\,z\in\mathcal{Z})=\left\{\sum_{i\in[n]}\alpha_{i}\,k(\cdot,z_{i}):\ n\in\mathbb{Z}^{>0},\ (z_{i})_{i=1}^{n}\subset\mathcal{Z},\ (\alpha_{i})_{i=1}^{n}\subset\mathbb{R}\right\}.

and consider
g=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}\,k(\cdot,z_{i})\ \in\ \mathcal{H}_{k,0}
.

–

h:=g\circ u\in\mathcal{H}_{k_{u}}
:
One has for any
x\in\mathcal{X}
that

\displaystyle h(x)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}g(u(x))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}\sum_{i\in[n]}\alpha_{i}k(u(x),z_{i})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{=}}\sum_{i\in[n]}\alpha_{i}k(u(x),u(x_{i}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{=}}\sum_{i\in[n]}\alpha_{i}k_{u}(x,x_{i}).

(88)

(a) holds by the definition of
h
, (b) follows from the definition of
g
, in (c) we used that
\mathcal{Z}=\operatorname{Im}(u)
hence there exist
\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}\subset\mathcal{X}
such that
z_{i}=u(x_{i})
for
i\in[n]
, (d) comes from the definition of
k_{u}
. Hence,
h=\sum_{i\in[n]}\alpha_{i}k_{u}(\cdot,x_{i})
, therefore
h\in\mathcal{H}_{k_{u}}
.

–

\|h\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k_{u}}}=\|g\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}
:

\displaystyle\|h\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k_{u}}}^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\left\langle\sum_{i\in[n]}\alpha_{i}k_{u}(\cdot,x_{i}),\sum_{j\in[n]}\alpha_{j}k_{u}(\cdot,x_{j})\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k_{u}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}\sum_{i,j\in[n]}\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}\left\langle k_{u}(\cdot,x_{i}),k_{u}(\cdot,x_{j})\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k_{u}}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{=}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}k_{u}(x_{i},x_{j})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{=}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}k(u(x_{i}),u(x_{j}))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{=}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}\langle k(\cdot,u(x_{i})),k(\cdot,u(x_{j}))\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}\left\langle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}k(\cdot,u(x_{i})),\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}k(\cdot,u(x_{j}))\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(e)}}{{=}}\left\langle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}k(\cdot,z_{i}),\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}k(\cdot,z_{j})\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(f)}}{{=}}\langle g,g\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(g)}}{{=}}\|g\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}^{2}.

In (a), we use (
88
) and the fact that in a Hilbert space the norm is induced by the inner product. (b) follows from the linearity of the inner product. The reproducing property implies (c); (d) is obtained by the definition of
k_{u}
. In (e) we use that
z_{i}=u(x_{i})
for all
i\in[n]
. (f) follows from the definition of
g
. (g) follows from the fact that in a Hilbert space the norm is induced by the inner product.

The results extend to
\mathcal{H}_{k}
due to the denseness of
\mathcal{H}_{k,0}
in
\mathcal{H}_{k}
.

•

Part-3
: Let
x\in\mathcal{X}
be arbitrary but fixed,
f(\cdot)=\alpha k_{u}(\cdot,x)
and
g(\cdot)=k_{\alpha u}(\cdot,x)
. Then for any
x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}
, one has

\displaystyle f(x^{\prime})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\alpha k_{u}(x^{\prime},x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}\alpha k^{(\mathrm{B})}(u(x^{\prime}),u(x))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{=}}k^{(\mathrm{B})}(\alpha u(x^{\prime}),\alpha u(x))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{=}}k_{\alpha u}(x^{\prime},x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(e)}}{{=}}g(x^{\prime}).

(a) and (b) follows from the definition of
f
and
k_{u}
, respectively, the non-negatively 1-homogeneous property of
k^{(\mathrm{B})}
gives (c), (d) and (e) is implied by the definition of
k_{\alpha u}
and
g
, respectively. This means that
f=g
.

∎

Lemma 17

(measure support on the sphere and kernel preservation)
.

Let the
L
-level BKL
\mathcal{H}^{(1:L)}_{\mu}
be defined according to Definition
1
under Assumption
3.1
, yielding

\displaystyle k^{(l)}=k\left[\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(l)},\,\mu^{(l)}\right],\qquad\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(l+1)}=\mathcal{H}_{k^{(l)}},\quad\text{for all }l\in[L-1].

Then, for each
l\in[L-1]
, there exists a probability measure

\hat{\mu}^{(l)}\in\mathcal{P}_{1,1}\left(\mathbb{S}_{1}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(l)}\right)\right)
such that

\displaystyle\hat{k}^{(l)}=k\left[\mathbb{S}_{1}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(l)}\right),\,\hat{\mu}^{(l)}\right],\qquad\mathcal{H}_{\hat{k}^{(l)}}=\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(l+1)},

and

\displaystyle\hat{k}^{(l)}=k^{(l)}\quad\text{pointwise on }\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X},\qquad\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(l+1)}=\mathcal{H}_{\hat{\mu}}^{(l+1)}.

Proof.

Fix
l\in[L-1]
.

Step-1
(removal of mass at the origin):
Let
\alpha\coloneqq\mu^{(l)}(\{0\})\in[0,1]
. If
\alpha=0
, we proceed directly. Otherwise, define the probability measure

\displaystyle\breve{\mu}^{(l)}(A)\coloneqq\frac{\mu^{(l)}\left(A\setminus\{0\}\right)}{1-\alpha},\qquad A\in\mathcal{A},

where
\mathcal{A}
denotes the Borel
\sigma
-algebra on
\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(l)}
. Then

\displaystyle\mu^{(l)}=(1-\alpha)\,\breve{\mu}^{(l)}+\alpha\,\delta_{0}.

Since
k^{(\mathrm{B})}(0,0)=0
, one has for all
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}
,

\displaystyle k^{(l)}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\int_{\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(l)}}k^{(\mathrm{B})}\left(u(\mathbf{x}),u(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\right)\mathrm{d}\mu^{(l)}(u)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}(1-\alpha)\int_{\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(l)}}k^{(\mathrm{B})}\left(u(\mathbf{x}),u(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\right)\mathrm{d}\breve{\mu}^{(l)}(u),

where (a) is the definition of
k^{(l)}
, and (b) follows from the decomposition of
\mu^{(l)}
and
k^{(\mathrm{B})}(0,0)=0
.
Hence, replacing
\mu^{(l)}
by
\breve{\mu}^{(l)}
scales the kernel by a positive constant. The associated RKHS coincides as a set, and its norm is rescaled by a multiplicative constant. Thus, without loss of generality, we assume
\mu^{(l)}(\{0\})=0
.

Step-2
(construction of the spherical measure):
Define the measurable map

\displaystyle N:\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(l)}\setminus\{0\}\to\mathbb{S}_{1}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(l)}\right),\qquad N(u)\coloneqq\frac{u}{\|u\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(l)}}},

and the finite measure
\tilde{\mu}^{(l)}
on
\mathbb{S}_{1}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(l)}\right)
by

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}_{1}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(l)}\right)}\varphi(v)\,d\tilde{\mu}^{(l)}(v)\coloneqq\int_{\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(l)}}\varphi\left(N(u)\right)\,\|u\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(l)}}\,d\mu^{(l)}(u),

for all bounded measurable
\varphi
. Then

\displaystyle\tilde{\mu}^{(l)}\left(\mathbb{S}_{1}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(l)}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\int_{\mathbb{S}_{1}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(l)}\right)}d\tilde{\mu}^{(l)}(v)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}\int_{\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(l)}}\|u\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(l)}}\,d\mu^{(l)}(u)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{=}}1,

where (a) is the definition of total mass, (b) follows from the definition of
\tilde{\mu}^{(l)}
, and (c) uses
\mu^{(l)}\in\mathcal{P}_{1,1}(\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(l)})
.
Thus
\tilde{\mu}^{(l)}\in\mathcal{P}_{1,1}(\mathbb{S}_{1}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(l)}\right))
. Define
\hat{\mu}^{(l)}\coloneqq\tilde{\mu}^{(l)}
.

Step-3
(equality of kernels):
For any
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}
, using
u=\|u\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(l)}}N(u)
, one has

\displaystyle k^{(l)}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\int_{\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(l)}}k^{(\mathrm{B})}\left(u(\mathbf{x}),u(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\right)\mathrm{d}\mu^{(l)}(u)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}\int_{\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(l)}}k^{(\mathrm{B})}\left(\|u\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(l)}}N(u)(\mathbf{x}),\|u\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(l)}}N(u)(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\right)\mathrm{d}\mu^{(l)}(u)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{=}}\int_{\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(l)}}\|u\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(l)}}\,k^{(\mathrm{B})}\left(N(u)(\mathbf{x}),N(u)(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\right)\mathrm{d}\mu^{(l)}(u)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{=}}\int_{\mathbb{S}_{1}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(l)}\right)}k^{(\mathrm{B})}\left(v(\mathbf{x}),v(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\right)\mathrm{d}\hat{\mu}^{(l)}(v),

where (a) is the definition of
k^{(l)}
, (b) rewrites
u
, (c) uses the positive
1
-homogeneity of
k^{(\mathrm{B})}
applied to scalar arguments
u(\mathbf{x})\in\mathbb{R}
, and (d) follows from the definition of the pushforward measure. Hence
k^{(l)}=\hat{k}^{(l)}
pointwise.

Step-4
(equality of RKHSs):
Since the kernels
k^{(l)}
and
\hat{k}^{(l)}
coincide pointwise, the associated RKHSs are identical with the same inner product. Hence
\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(l+1)}=\mathcal{H}_{\hat{\mu}}^{(l+1)}
. This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 18

(bounded Radon-Nikodym derivative and norm inequality)
.

Let the
L
-level BKLs
\mathcal{H}_{\mu_{a}}^{(1:L)}
and
\mathcal{H}_{\mu_{b}}^{(1:L)}
be as introduced in Definition
1
with
\mathcal{S}_{\mu_{a}}^{(l)}\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{\mu_{a}}^{(l)}
and
\mathcal{S}_{\mu_{b}}^{(l)}\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{\mu_{b}}^{(l)}
and associated measures
\mu_{a}^{(l)}\in\mathcal{P}_{1}\left(\mathcal{S}_{\mu_{a}}^{(l)}\right)
and
\mu_{b}^{(l)}\in\mathcal{P}_{1}\left(\mathcal{S}_{\mu_{b}}^{(l)}\right)
for all
l\in[L-1]
. Then, for
l\in[L-1]
, one has

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\mu_{a}}^{(l+1)}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{H}_{k_{a}^{(l)}},\qquad k_{a}^{(l)}=k\left[\mathcal{S}_{\mu_{a}}^{(l)},\,\mu_{a}^{(l)}\right],

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\mu_{b}}^{(l+1)}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{H}_{k_{b}^{(l)}},\qquad k_{b}^{(l)}=k\left[\mathcal{S}_{\mu_{b}}^{(l)},\,\mu_{b}^{(l)}\right].

Assume moreover that
\mu_{b}^{(l)}\ll\mu_{a}^{(l)}
with Radon–Nikodym derivative

\displaystyle w(u)\coloneqq\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu_{b}^{(l)}}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{a}^{(l)}}(u)\quad\text{satisfying}\quad 0\leq w(u)\leq M\quad\mu_{a}^{(l)}\text{-a.e.}

for some constant
M>0
. Then, for every
l\in[L-1]
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\mu_{b}}^{(l+1)}\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{\mu_{a}}^{(l+1)}\quad\text{and}\quad\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\mu_{a}}^{(l+1)}}\leq\sqrt{M}\,\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\mu_{b}}^{(l+1)}},\qquad\forall f\in\mathcal{H}_{\mu_{b}}^{(l+1)}.

Proof.

Fix
l\in[L-1]
and let
f\in\mathcal{H}_{\mu_{b}}^{(l+1)}
. Let
\epsilon>0
be arbitrary. By
\Cref
coro:IRKHS_BKL,

\displaystyle f(\mathbf{x})=\int_{\mathcal{S}_{\mu_{b}}^{(l)}}g_{u}^{(l)}\left(u(\mathbf{x})\right)\mathrm{d}\mu_{b}^{(l)}(u),\quad\text{for all }\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X},

where
g_{u}^{(l)}\in\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}
for all
u\in\mathcal{S}_{\mu_{b}}^{(l)}
, with the norm defined in (
87
). Moreover, since the RKHS norm is defined via an infimum, we may select an
\epsilon
-optimal representation

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{S}_{\mu_{b}}^{(l)}}\|g_{u}^{(l)}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}}^{2}\mathrm{d}\mu_{b}^{(l)}(u)\leq\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\mu_{b}}^{(l+1)}}^{2}+\epsilon.

(89)

Since
\mu_{b}^{(l)}\ll\mu_{a}^{(l)}
with Radon–Nikodym derivative
w=\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu_{b}^{(l)}}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{a}^{(l)}}
, we can express

\displaystyle f(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{S}_{\mu_{b}}^{(l)}}g_{u}^{(l)}\left(u(\mathbf{x})\right)\mathrm{d}\mu_{b}^{(l)}(u)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\int_{\mathcal{S}_{\mu_{b}}^{(l)}}g_{u}^{(l)}\left(u(\mathbf{x})\right)\,w(u)\mathrm{d}\mu_{a}^{(l)}(u)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}\int_{\mathcal{S}_{\mu_{a}}^{(l)}}h_{u}^{(l)}\left(u(\mathbf{x})\right)\mathrm{d}\mu_{a}^{(l)}(u),

where (a) applies the change of measure
\mathrm{d}\mu_{b}^{(l)}=w(u)\mathrm{d}\mu_{a}^{(l)}

and (b) defines
h_{u}^{(l)}:=w(u)\,g_{u}^{(l)}\in\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}
, which yields a valid representation of
f
with respect to
\mu_{a}^{(l)}
.
Hence
f\in\mathcal{H}_{\mu_{a}}^{(l+1)}
. Thus

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{S}_{\mu_{a}}^{(l)}}\|h_{u}^{(l)}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}}^{2}\mathrm{d}\mu_{a}^{(l)}(u)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\int_{\mathcal{S}_{\mu_{a}}^{(l)}}w(u)^{2}\,\|g_{u}^{(l)}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}}^{2}\mathrm{d}\mu_{a}^{(l)}(u)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\leq}}M\int_{\mathcal{S}_{\mu_{a}}^{(l)}}w(u)\,\|g_{u}^{(l)}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}}^{2}\mathrm{d}\mu_{a}^{(l)}(u)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{=}}M\int_{\mathcal{S}_{\mu_{b}}^{(l)}}\|g_{u}^{(l)}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}}^{2}\mathrm{d}\mu_{b}^{(l)}(u)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{\leq}}M\left(\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\mu_{b}}^{(l+1)}}^{2}+\epsilon\right).

Here (a) applies the definition
h_{u}^{(l)}=w(u)g_{u}^{(l)}
, (b) uses the boundedness
0\leq w(u)\leq M
, giving
w(u)^{2}\leq Mw(u)
for
\mu_{a}^{(l)}
-a.e.
u
, (c) applies again the change of measure
\mathrm{d}\mu_{b}^{(l)}=w\mathrm{d}\mu_{a}^{(l)}
, and (d) invokes the near-minimal property (
89
). Finally, since the RKHS norm in
\mathcal{H}_{\mu_{a}}^{(l+1)}

is defined as the infimum over all such representations, we have

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\mu_{a}}^{(l+1)}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathcal{S}_{\mu_{a}}^{(l)}}\|h_{u}^{(l)}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}}^{2}\mathrm{d}\mu_{a}^{(l)}(u)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\leq}}M\left(\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\mu_{b}}^{(l+1)}}^{2}+\epsilon\right).

Letting
\epsilon\downarrow 0
yields
\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\mu_{a}}^{(l+1)}}\leq\sqrt{M}\,\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\mu_{b}}^{(l+1)}}
. Since
f\in\mathcal{H}_{\mu_{b}}^{(l+1)}
was arbitrary, we conclude both the inclusion

\mathcal{H}_{\mu_{b}}^{(l+1)}\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{\mu_{a}}^{(l+1)}

and the claimed norm inequality.
∎

Lemma 19

(total variation convergence of hierarchical mixtures)
.

For all
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{>0}
, let an
L
-level BKL
\mathcal{H}_{\mu_{j}}^{(1:L)}
is defined according to
\Cref
def:BKLdef with
\mathcal{S}_{\mu_{j}}^{(l)}\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{\hat{\mu}_{j}}^{(L)}
and
\mu_{j}^{(l)}\in\mathcal{P}_{1}\left(\mathcal{S}_{\mu_{j}}^{(l)}\right)
for all
l\in[L-1]
. One has

\displaystyle k_{j}^{(l)}=k\left[\mathcal{S}_{\mu_{j}}^{(l)},\,\mu_{j}^{(l)}\right],\quad\mathcal{H}_{\mu_{j}}^{(l+1)}=\mathcal{H}_{k_{j}^{(l)}},\quad\text{for each }l\in[L-1].

Fix a layer
l\in[L-1]
, and consider the RKHS
\mathcal{H}_{\mu_{j}}^{(l)}

equipped with its Borel
\sigma
-algebra.
Define the hierarchical mixture measure

\displaystyle\mu_{s}^{(l)}\coloneqq(2^{l}-1)\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{-(j+1)l}\,\mu_{j}^{(l)},\quad\text{for all }l\in[L-1].

Then the following statements hold.

1.

The series defining
\mu_{s}^{(l)}
converges in total variation.
Moreover,
\mu_{s}^{(l)}
is a probability measure with unit first moment:

\displaystyle\mu_{s}^{(l)}\in\mathcal{P}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\mu_{j}}^{(l)}\right),\quad\text{for all }l\in[L-1].

2.

Each measure
\mu_{j}^{(l)}
is absolutely continuous with respect to

\mu_{s}^{(l)}
, and its Radon–Nikodym derivative satisfies the uniform bound

\displaystyle 0\leq\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu_{j}^{(l)}}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{s}^{(l)}}(u)\leq\frac{2^{(j+1)l}}{2^{l}-1},\qquad\text{for $\mu_{s}^{(l)}$-a.e.\ }u\in\mathcal{H}_{\mu_{j}}^{(l)}\text{ and for all }l\in[L-1].

In particular, for all
l\in[L-1]
,
\mu_{s}^{(l)}
dominates the family

\left\{\mu_{j}^{(l)}\right\}_{j\geq 0}
with explicit Radon–Nikodym control.

Proof.

Fix
l\in[L-1]
and write
w_{j}:=2^{-(j+1)l}
so that
\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}w_{j}=\frac{1}{2^{l}-1}
.

1.

(convergence in total variation and normalization):Consider the partial sums
S_{n}:=\sum_{j=0}^{n}w_{j}\,\mu_{j}^{(l)}
and the tail

\displaystyle T_{n}:=\sum_{j=n+1}^{\infty}w_{j}\,\mu_{j}^{(l)}.

Then

\displaystyle\|T_{n}\|_{TV}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\leq}}\sum_{j=n+1}^{\infty}w_{j}\,\|\mu_{j}^{(l)}\|_{TV}\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\leq}}\ \left(\sup_{j\geq 0}\|\mu_{j}^{(l)}\|_{TV}\right)\sum_{j=n+1}^{\infty}w_{j}\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{\longrightarrow}}\ 0\quad(n\to\infty),

where
(a) uses the triangle inequality for the TV norm and nonnegativity of the weights
w_{j}
, (b) pulls out a uniform bound, and (c) uses the geometric decay
\sum_{j=n+1}^{\infty}w_{j}\to 0
.
Thus
(S_{n})_{n\geq 0}
is Cauchy in total variation and converges to a finite measure
S:=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}w_{j}\,\mu_{j}^{(l)}
. Multiplying by
(2^{l}-1)
preserves convergence, hence
\mu_{s}^{(l)}=(2^{l}-1)S
is well defined and finite. If additionally each
\mu_{j}^{(l)}
is a probability measure, then

\displaystyle\mu_{s}^{(l)}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\mu_{j}}^{(l)}\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}(2^{l}-1)\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}w_{j}\,\mu_{j}^{(l)}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\mu_{j}}^{(l)}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}(2^{l}-1)\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}w_{j}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{=}}(2^{l}-1)\cdot\frac{1}{2^{l}-1}=1,

where (a) is the definition of
\mu_{s}^{(l)}
, (b) uses
\mu_{j}^{(l)}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\mu_{j}}^{(l)}\right)=1
, and (c) sums the geometric series. Hence
\mu_{s}^{(l)}\in\mathcal{P}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\mu_{j}}^{(l)}\right)
.

2.

(absolute continuity and Radon–Nikodym bound): Fix
j\geq 0
. For any measurable
A\in\mathcal{A}
,

\displaystyle\mu_{s}^{(l)}(A)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}(2^{l}-1)\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}w_{j}\,\mu_{j}^{(l)}(A)\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\geq}}\ (2^{l}-1)\,w_{j}\,\mu_{j}^{(l)}(A)\ =\ \frac{2^{l}-1}{2^{(j+1)l}}\,\mu_{j}^{(l)}(A).

Here (a) is the definition of
\mu_{s}^{(l)}
, (b) drops all nonnegative summands except
j=j
.
Thus, if
\mu_{s}^{(l)}(A)=0
then necessarily
\mu_{j}^{(l)}(A)=0
. Hence
\mu_{j}^{(l)}\ll\mu_{s}^{(l)}
. Moreover, for all measurable
A
we have the domination

\displaystyle\mu_{j}^{(l)}(A)\ \leq\ \frac{2^{(j+1)l}}{2^{l}-1}\,\mu_{s}^{(l)}(A).

By the Radon–Nikodym theorem, this implies that the density exists and is essentially bounded:

\displaystyle\frac{d\mu_{j}^{(l)}}{d\mu_{s}^{(l)}}(u)\ \leq\ \frac{2^{(j+1)l}}{2^{l}-1}\quad\text{for $\mu_{s}^{(l)}$-a.e.\ $u\in\mathcal{H}_{\mu_{j}}^{(l)}$.}

This proves
2
.

∎

Lemma 20

(line segment implies

\Cref
thm:charachteriz_funspace
3
)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be compact, and assume that there exist

\mathbf{v}_{1},\mathbf{v}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\mathbf{v}_{1}\neq\mathbf{v}_{2}
such that

\displaystyle[\mathbf{v}_{1},\mathbf{v}_{2}]:=\{(1-t)\mathbf{v}_{1}+t\mathbf{v}_{2}:t\in[0,1]\}\subseteq\mathcal{X}.

Then the assumption in

\Cref
thm:charachteriz_funspace
3

is satisfied.

Proof.

We show that the existence of a non-degenerate line segment

\displaystyle[\mathbf{v}_{1},\mathbf{v}_{2}]=\left\{(1-t)\mathbf{v}_{1}+t\mathbf{v}_{2}:\ t\in[0,1]\right\}\subseteq\mathcal{X}

implies the geometric trace condition appearing in

\Cref
thm:charachteriz_funspace
3
,
after recentering at the point
\mathbf{x}_{0}=\mathbf{v}_{1}
.

Step-1
(choice of
\mathbf{x}_{0}
and
\gamma
):
Define

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}_{0}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathbf{v}_{1},

\displaystyle\gamma(t)

\displaystyle\coloneqq(1-t)\mathbf{v}_{1}+t\mathbf{v}_{2},\qquad t\in[0,1].

We rewrite
\gamma
in a more convenient form:

\displaystyle\gamma(t)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}(1-t)\mathbf{v}_{1}+t\mathbf{v}_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}\mathbf{v}_{1}-t\mathbf{v}_{1}+t\mathbf{v}_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{=}}\mathbf{v}_{1}+t(\mathbf{v}_{2}-\mathbf{v}_{1}).

Here (a) holds by the definition of
\gamma
, (b) is obtained by distributing the scalar multiplication, and (c) results from factoring out the common scalar
t
. Since
[\mathbf{v}_{1},\mathbf{v}_{2}]\subseteq\mathcal{X}
by assumption, we obtain

\displaystyle\gamma(t)\in\mathcal{X},\qquad\forall t\in[0,1],

which guarantees that
\gamma:[0,1]\to\mathcal{X}
is well defined. Moreover,

\displaystyle\gamma(0)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}(1-0)\mathbf{v}_{1}+0\cdot\mathbf{v}_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}\mathbf{v}_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{=}}\mathbf{x}_{0}.

Here (a) follows from the definition of
\gamma
, (b) uses elementary properties of scalar multiplication, and (c) reflects the definition of
\mathbf{x}_{0}
. Thus
\gamma(0)=\mathbf{x}_{0}
.

Step-2
(
\gamma
is Lipschitz):
Let
s,t\in[0,1]
be arbitrary. Then

\displaystyle\gamma(t)-\gamma(s)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\left(\mathbf{v}_{1}+t(\mathbf{v}_{2}-\mathbf{v}_{1})\right)-\left(\mathbf{v}_{1}+s(\mathbf{v}_{2}-\mathbf{v}_{1})\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}(t-s)(\mathbf{v}_{2}-\mathbf{v}_{1}),

where (a) uses the representation obtained in Step-1, while (b) comes from cancelling
\mathbf{v}_{1}
and extracting
(t-s)
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\left\|\gamma(t)-\gamma(s)\right\|_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\left\|(t-s)(\mathbf{v}_{2}-\mathbf{v}_{1})\right\|_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}\left|t-s\right|\,\left\|\mathbf{v}_{2}-\mathbf{v}_{1}\right\|_{2}.

Here (a) is a direct substitution, whereas (b) follows from the absolute homogeneity of the Euclidean norm. Hence

\displaystyle\left\|\gamma(t)-\gamma(s)\right\|_{2}\leq\left\|\mathbf{v}_{2}-\mathbf{v}_{1}\right\|_{2}\left|t-s\right|,\qquad\forall s,t\in[0,1],

which shows that
\gamma
is Lipschitz.

Step-3
(choice of the affine functional):
Since
\mathbf{v}_{1}\neq\mathbf{v}_{2}
, we have
\left\|\mathbf{v}_{2}-\mathbf{v}_{1}\right\|_{2}^{2}>0
, which ensures that the vector
\mathbf{v}_{2}-\mathbf{v}_{1}/\left\|\mathbf{v}_{2}-\mathbf{v}_{1}\right\|_{2}^{2}
is well defined. Define

\displaystyle u_{1}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\langle\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{v}_{1},\frac{\mathbf{v}_{2}-\mathbf{v}_{1}}{\left\|\mathbf{v}_{2}-\mathbf{v}_{1}\right\|_{2}^{2}}\right\rangle,\qquad\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}.

We verify

\displaystyle u_{1}(\mathbf{x}_{0})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}u_{1}(\mathbf{v}_{1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}\left\langle\mathbf{v}_{1}-\mathbf{v}_{1},\frac{\mathbf{v}_{2}-\mathbf{v}_{1}}{\left\|\mathbf{v}_{2}-\mathbf{v}_{1}\right\|_{2}^{2}}\right\rangle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{=}}\left\langle\mathbf{0},\frac{\mathbf{v}_{2}-\mathbf{v}_{1}}{\left\|\mathbf{v}_{2}-\mathbf{v}_{1}\right\|_{2}^{2}}\right\rangle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{=}}0.

Here (a) reflects the definition of
\mathbf{x}_{0}
, (b) applies the definition of
u_{1}
, (c) uses
\mathbf{v}_{1}-\mathbf{v}_{1}=\mathbf{0}
, and (d) follows from the property
\langle\mathbf{0},w\rangle=0
.

Step-4
(linear growth along
\gamma
):
Let
t\in[0,1]
. Then

\displaystyle u_{1}(\gamma(t))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\left\langle\gamma(t)-\mathbf{v}_{1},\frac{\mathbf{v}_{2}-\mathbf{v}_{1}}{\left\|\mathbf{v}_{2}-\mathbf{v}_{1}\right\|_{2}^{2}}\right\rangle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}\left\langle\mathbf{v}_{1}+t(\mathbf{v}_{2}-\mathbf{v}_{1})-\mathbf{v}_{1},\frac{\mathbf{v}_{2}-\mathbf{v}_{1}}{\left\|\mathbf{v}_{2}-\mathbf{v}_{1}\right\|_{2}^{2}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{=}}\left\langle t(\mathbf{v}_{2}-\mathbf{v}_{1}),\frac{\mathbf{v}_{2}-\mathbf{v}_{1}}{\left\|\mathbf{v}_{2}-\mathbf{v}_{1}\right\|_{2}^{2}}\right\rangle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{=}}t\left\langle\mathbf{v}_{2}-\mathbf{v}_{1},\frac{\mathbf{v}_{2}-\mathbf{v}_{1}}{\left\|\mathbf{v}_{2}-\mathbf{v}_{1}\right\|_{2}^{2}}\right\rangle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(e)}}{{=}}t\frac{\left\langle\mathbf{v}_{2}-\mathbf{v}_{1},\mathbf{v}_{2}-\mathbf{v}_{1}\right\rangle}{\left\|\mathbf{v}_{2}-\mathbf{v}_{1}\right\|_{2}^{2}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(f)}}{{=}}t\frac{\left\|\mathbf{v}_{2}-\mathbf{v}_{1}\right\|_{2}^{2}}{\left\|\mathbf{v}_{2}-\mathbf{v}_{1}\right\|_{2}^{2}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(g)}}{{=}}t.

Here (a) uses the definition of
u_{1}
, (b) substitutes
\gamma(t)
, (c) cancels
\mathbf{v}_{1}
, (d) exploits linearity of the inner product, (e) rewrites the scalar factor, (f) invokes
\langle w,w\rangle=\|w\|_{2}^{2}
, and (g) simplifies the ratio.

Step-5
(linearization):
Define

\displaystyle\tilde{u}_{1}(\mathbf{x})\coloneqq\left\langle\mathbf{x},\frac{\mathbf{v}_{2}-\mathbf{v}_{1}}{\left\|\mathbf{v}_{2}-\mathbf{v}_{1}\right\|_{2}^{2}}\right\rangle.

Then
\tilde{u}_{1}\in\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(1)}
.

Step-6
(increment identity):

\displaystyle\tilde{u}_{1}(\gamma(t))-\tilde{u}_{1}(\mathbf{x}_{0})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\left\langle\gamma(t)-\mathbf{x}_{0},\frac{\mathbf{v}_{2}-\mathbf{v}_{1}}{\left\|\mathbf{v}_{2}-\mathbf{v}_{1}\right\|_{2}^{2}}\right\rangle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}\left\langle\gamma(t)-\mathbf{v}_{1},\frac{\mathbf{v}_{2}-\mathbf{v}_{1}}{\left\|\mathbf{v}_{2}-\mathbf{v}_{1}\right\|_{2}^{2}}\right\rangle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{=}}u_{1}(\gamma(t))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{=}}t.

Here (a) combines the two terms, (b) uses
\mathbf{x}_{0}=\mathbf{v}_{1}
, (c) invokes the definition of
u_{1}
, and (d) follows from Step-4. We have constructed
\mathbf{x}_{0}
,
\gamma
, and
\tilde{u}_{1}\in\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(1)}
such that

\displaystyle\tilde{u}_{1}(\gamma(t))-\tilde{u}_{1}(\mathbf{x}_{0})=t,\qquad\forall t\in[0,1],

which establishes the required geometric condition in

\Cref
thm:charachteriz_funspace
3
.
∎

Lemma 21

(simplified empirical Gaussian complexity)
.

Let us apply
\Cref
assumption:normalizing. Fix
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{>0}
, a dataset
\{\mathbf{x}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}\subset\mathcal{X}
, and let

\bm{\varepsilon}=(\varepsilon_{i})_{i=1}^{n}\sim\mathcal{N}^{n}(0,1)
. For any
r>0
, the Gaussian complexity of the set
\mathcal{B}^{(L)}_{r}
satisfies

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{n}\left(\mathcal{B}^{(L)}_{r}\right)\leq r\mathcal{G}_{n}\left(\mathcal{B}^{(L)}_{1}\right).

Proof.

Let
f\in\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{(L)}
and
r>0
. By the canonical ladder construction, for all
l\in[L-1]
there exists a probability measure
\hat{\mu}^{(l)}\in\mathcal{P}_{1,1}\left(\mathbb{S}_{1}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(l)}\right)\right)
such that

\displaystyle\hat{k}^{(l)}=k\left[\mathbb{S}_{1}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(l)}\right),\hat{\mu}^{(l)}\right],\qquad\mathcal{H}_{\hat{\mu}}^{(l+1)}=\mathcal{H}_{\hat{k}^{(l)}},\quad f\in\mathcal{H}_{\hat{\mu}}^{(L)}.

In particular,

\displaystyle\widehat{C}^{(L)}(f)=\inf_{\mathcal{H}^{(1:L)}_{\hat{\mu}}\in\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{(1:L)}\,:\,f\in\mathcal{H}_{\hat{\mu}}^{(L)}}\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\hat{\mu}}^{(L)}},\quad\forall f\in\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{(L)}.

From the definition of the Gaussian complexity in
(
27
), it follows that

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{n}\left(\mathcal{B}^{(L)}_{r}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\leq}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{N}^{n}(0,1),\nu_{X}^{n}}\left[\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\hat{\mu}^{(1:L-1)}\text{ s.t. }\\[2.0pt]
\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\hat{\mu}}^{(L)}}\leq r\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}\int_{\mathcal{S}_{1}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(L-1)}\right)}g_{u}^{(L-1)}(u(\mathbf{x}_{i}))\mathrm{d}\mu^{(L-1)}(u)\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{N}^{n}(0,1),\nu_{X}^{n}}\left[\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\hat{\mu}^{(1:L-1)}\text{ s.t. }\\[2.0pt]
\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\hat{\mu}}^{(L)}}\leq r\end{subarray}}\int_{\mathcal{S}_{1}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(L-1)}\right)}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}\frac{g_{u}^{(L-1)}(u(\mathbf{x}_{i}))}{\|g_{u}^{(L-1)}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}}}\vphantom{\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\hat{\mu}^{(1:L-1)}\text{ s.t. }\\[2.0pt]
\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\hat{\mu}}^{(L)}}\leq r\end{subarray}}\int_{\mathcal{S}_{1}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(L-1)}\right)}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}\frac{g_{u}^{(L-1)}(u(\mathbf{x}_{i}))}{\|g_{u}^{(L-1)}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}}}}{}\right.

\displaystyle\left.\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\vphantom{\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\hat{\mu}^{(1:L-1)}\text{ s.t. }\\[2.0pt]
\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\hat{\mu}}^{(L)}}\leq r\end{subarray}}\int_{\mathcal{S}_{1}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(L-1)}\right)}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}\frac{g_{u}^{(L-1)}(u(\mathbf{x}_{i}))}{}\mathrm{d}\mu^{(L-1)}(u)}\cdot\mathds{1}_{\left\{g_{u}^{(L-1)}\neq 0\right\}}\|g_{u}^{(L-1)}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}}\mathrm{d}\mu^{(L-1)}(u)\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{=}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{N}^{n}(0,1),\nu_{X}^{n}}\left[\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\hat{\mu}^{(1:L-1)}\text{ s.t. }\\[2.0pt]
\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\hat{\mu}}^{(L)}}\leq r\end{subarray}}\int_{\mathcal{S}_{1}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(L-1)}\right)}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}\frac{\left\langle g_{u}^{(L-1)},k_{u(\mathbf{x}_{i})}^{(B)}\right\rangle}{\|g_{u}^{(L-1)}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}}}\vphantom{\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\hat{\mu}^{(1:L-1)}\text{ s.t. }\\[2.0pt]
\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\hat{\mu}}^{(L)}}\leq r\end{subarray}}\int_{\mathcal{S}_{1}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(L-1)}\right)}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}\frac{\left\langle g_{u}^{(L-1)},k_{u(\mathbf{x}_{i})}^{(B)}\right\rangle}{\|g_{u}^{(L-1)}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}}}}{}\right.

\displaystyle\left.\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\vphantom{\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\hat{\mu}^{(1:L-1)}\text{ s.t. }\\[2.0pt]
\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\hat{\mu}}^{(L)}}\leq r\end{subarray}}\int_{\mathcal{S}_{1}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(L-1)}\right)}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}\frac{\left\langle g_{u}^{(L-1)},k_{u(\mathbf{x}_{i})}^{(B)}\right\rangle}{\|g_{u}^{(L-1)}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}}}}{}\cdot\mathds{1}_{\left\{g_{u}^{(L-1)}\neq 0\right\}}\|g_{u}^{(L-1)}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}}\mathrm{d}\mu^{(L-1)}(u)\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{\leq}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{N}^{n}(0,1),\nu_{X}^{n}}\left[\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\hat{\mu}^{(1:L-1)}\text{ s.t. }\\[2.0pt]
\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\hat{\mu}}^{(L)}}\leq r\end{subarray}}\bigintsss_{\mathcal{S}_{1}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(L-1)}\right)}\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}\frac{\left\langle g_{u}^{(L-1)},k_{u(\mathbf{x}_{i})}^{(B)}\right\rangle}{\|g_{u}^{(L-1)}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}}}\right|\vphantom{\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\hat{\mu}^{(1:L-1)}\text{ s.t. }\\[2.0pt]
\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\hat{\mu}}^{(L)}}\leq r\end{subarray}}\bigintsss_{\mathcal{S}_{1}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(L-1)}\right)}\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}\frac{\left\langle g_{u}^{(L-1)},k_{u(\mathbf{x}_{i})}^{(B)}\right\rangle}{\|g_{u}^{(L-1)}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}}}\right|}{}\right.

\displaystyle\left.\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\vphantom{\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\hat{\mu}^{(1:L-1)}\text{ s.t. }\\[2.0pt]
\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\hat{\mu}}^{(L)}}\leq r\end{subarray}}\bigintsss_{\mathcal{S}_{1}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(L-1)}\right)}\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}\frac{\left\langle g_{u}^{(L-1)},k_{u(\mathbf{x}_{i})}^{(B)}\right\rangle}{\|g_{u}^{(L-1)}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}}}\right|}{}\cdot\mathds{1}_{\left\{g_{u}^{(L-1)}\neq 0\right\}}\|g_{u}^{(L-1)}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}}\mathrm{d}\mu^{(L-1)}(u)\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(e)}}{{\leq}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{N}^{n}(0,1),\nu_{X}^{n}}\left[\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\hat{\mu}^{(1:L-1)}\text{ s.t. }\\[2.0pt]
\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\hat{\mu}}^{(L)}}\leq r\end{subarray}}\sup_{u\in\mathcal{S}_{1}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(L-1)}\right)}\sup_{g_{u}^{(L-1)}\in\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}}\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}\frac{\left\langle g_{u}^{(L-1)},k_{u(\mathbf{x}_{i})}^{(B)}\right\rangle}{\|g_{u}^{(L-1)}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}}}\right|\vphantom{\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\hat{\mu}^{(1:L-1)}\text{ s.t. }\\[2.0pt]
\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\hat{\mu}}^{(L)}}\leq r\end{subarray}}\sup_{u\in\mathcal{S}_{1}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(L-1)}\right)}\sup_{g_{u}^{(L-1)}\in\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}}\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}\frac{\left\langle g_{u}^{(L-1)},k_{u(\mathbf{x}_{i})}^{(B)}\right\rangle}{\|g_{u}^{(L-1)}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}}}\right|}{}\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\left.\vphantom{\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\hat{\mu}^{(1:L-1)}\text{ s.t. }\\[2.0pt]
\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\hat{\mu}}^{(L)}}\leq r\end{subarray}}\sup_{u\in\mathcal{S}_{1}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(L-1)}\right)}\sup_{g_{u}^{(L-1)}\in\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}}\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}\frac{\left\langle g_{u}^{(L-1)},k_{u(\mathbf{x}_{i})}^{(B)}\right\rangle}{\|g_{u}^{(L-1)}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}}}\right|}{}\cdot\int_{\mathcal{S}_{1}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(L-1)}\right)}\mathds{1}_{\left\{g_{u}^{(L-1)}\neq 0\right\}}\|g_{u}^{(L-1)}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}}\mathrm{d}\mu^{(L-1)}(u)\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(f)}}{{\leq}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{N}^{n}(0,1),\nu_{X}^{n}}\left[\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\hat{\mu}^{(1:L-1)}\text{ s.t. }\\[2.0pt]
\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\hat{\mu}}^{(L)}}\leq r\end{subarray}}\sup_{u\in\mathcal{S}_{1}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(L-1)}\right)}\sup_{g_{u}^{(L-1)}\in\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}}\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}\frac{\left\langle g_{u}^{(L-1)},k_{u(\mathbf{x}_{i})}^{(B)}\right\rangle}{\|g_{u}^{(L-1)}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}}}\right|\vphantom{\int_{\mathcal{S}_{1}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(L-1)}\right)}\mathds{1}_{\left\{g_{u}^{(L-1)}\neq 0\right\}}\|g_{u}^{(L-1)}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}}\mathrm{d}\mu^{(L-1)}(u)}\vphantom{\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\hat{\mu}^{(1:L-1)}\text{ s.t. }\\[2.0pt]
\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\hat{\mu}}^{(L)}}\leq r\end{subarray}}\sup_{u\in\mathcal{S}_{1}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(L-1)}\right)}\sup_{g_{u}^{(L-1)}\in\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}}\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}\frac{\left\langle g_{u}^{(L-1)},k_{u(\mathbf{x}_{i})}^{(B)}\right\rangle}{\|g_{u}^{(L-1)}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}}}\right|\vphantom{\int_{\mathcal{S}_{1}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(L-1)}\right)}\mathds{1}_{\left\{g_{u}^{(L-1)}\neq 0\right\}}\|g_{u}^{(L-1)}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}}\mathrm{d}\mu^{(L-1)}(u)}}{}\right.

\displaystyle\left.\vphantom{\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\hat{\mu}^{(1:L-1)}\text{ s.t. }\\[2.0pt]
\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\hat{\mu}}^{(L)}}\leq r\end{subarray}}\sup_{u\in\mathcal{S}_{1}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(L-1)}\right)}\sup_{g_{u}^{(L-1)}\in\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}}\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}\frac{\left\langle g_{u}^{(L-1)},k_{u(\mathbf{x}_{i})}^{(B)}\right\rangle}{\|g_{u}^{(L-1)}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}}}\right|\vphantom{\int_{\mathcal{S}_{1}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(L-1)}\right)}\mathds{1}_{\left\{g_{u}^{(L-1)}\neq 0\right\}}\|g_{u}^{(L-1)}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}}\mathrm{d}\mu^{(L-1)}(u)}}{}\cdot\left(\int_{\mathcal{S}_{1}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(L-1)}\right)}\mathds{1}_{\left\{g_{u}^{(L-1)}\neq 0\right\}}\|g_{u}^{(L-1)}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}}^{2}\mathrm{d}\mu^{(L-1)}(u)\right)^{1/2}\left(\int_{\mathcal{S}_{1}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(L-1)}\right)}\mathrm{d}\mu^{(L-1)}(u)\right)^{1/2}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(g)}}{{\leq}}r\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{N}^{n}(0,1),\nu_{X}^{n}}\left[\sup_{g^{(L-1)}\in\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}}\sup_{u\in\mathcal{S}_{1}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(L-1)}\right)}\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}\left\langle\frac{g^{(L-1)}}{\|g^{(L-1)}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}}},k_{u(\mathbf{x}_{i})}^{(B)}\right\rangle\right|\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(h)}}{{=}}r\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{N}^{n}(0,1),\nu_{X}^{n}}\left[\sup_{\|\hat{g}^{(L-1)}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k^{(B)}}}=1}\sup_{\|u\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(L-1)}}=1}\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}\left\langle\hat{g}^{(L-1)},k_{u(\mathbf{x}_{i})}^{(B)}\right\rangle\right|\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(i)}}{{\leq}}r\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{N}^{n}(0,1),\nu_{X}^{n}}\left[\sup_{\|\hat{g}^{(L-1)}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k^{(B)}}}\leq 1}\sup_{\|u\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(L-1)}}\leq 1}\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}\left\langle\hat{g}^{(L-1)},k_{u(\mathbf{x}_{i})}^{(B)}\right\rangle\right|\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(j)}}{{=}}r\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{N}^{n}(0,1),\nu_{X}^{n}}\left[\sup_{\|\hat{g}^{(L-1)}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k^{(B)}}}\leq 1}\sup_{\|u\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(L-1)}}\leq 1}\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}\hat{g}^{(L-1)}(u(X))\right|\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(k)}}{{\leq}}r\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{N}^{n}(0,1),\nu_{X}^{n}}\left[\sup_{\|\hat{g}^{(L-1)}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k^{(B)}}}\leq 1}\sup_{\|u\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(L-1)}}\leq 1}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}\hat{g}^{(L-1)}(u(X))\right],

Hence, there is

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{n}\left(\mathcal{B}^{(L)}_{r}\right)\leq r\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{N}^{n}(0,1),\nu_{X}^{n}}\left[\sup_{\|\hat{g}^{(L-1)}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k^{(B)}}}\leq 1}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\hat{\mu}^{(1:L-2)}\text{ s.t. }\\[2.0pt]
\|u\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(L-1)}}\end{subarray}\leq 1}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}\hat{g}^{(L-1)}(u(\mathbf{x}_{i}))\right]

\displaystyle=r\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{N}^{n}(0,1),\nu_{X}^{n}}\left[\sup_{\|\hat{g}^{(L-1)}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}}\leq 1}\sup_{\widehat{\mathit{C}}^{(L-1)}(u)\leq 1}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}\hat{g}^{(L-1)}(u(\mathbf{x}_{i}))\right]

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{aligned} &r\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{N}^{n}(0,1),\nu_{X}^{n}}\left[\sup_{\|\hat{g}^{(L-1)}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}}\leq 1}\sup_{\widehat{\mathit{C}}^{(L-1)}(u)\leq 1}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}\hat{g}^{(L-1)}(u(\mathbf{x}_{i}))\right],\,&\text{if}\,\,\,L\geq 3,\\
&r\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{N}^{n}(0,1),\nu_{X}^{n}}\left[\sup_{\|\hat{g}^{(2)}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}}\leq 1}\sup_{\widehat{C}^{(2)}(u)\leq 1}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}\hat{g}^{(2)}(u(\mathbf{x}_{i}))\right],\,&\text{if}\,\,\,L=2.\end{aligned}\right.

This proves the claim.
Here (a)–(k) are justified as follows:
(a) enlarges the admissible class

\left\{f:\widehat{C}^{(L)}(f)\leq r\right\}
to
\left\{f:\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\hat{\mu}}^{(L)}}\leq r\right\}
via the representation of
f
in
\mathcal{H}^{(1:L)}_{\mu}
, which increases the supremum;
(b) factors out the normalization term

\left\|g_{u}^{(L-1)}\right\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}}
inside the integral;
(c) follows from the reproducing property of
\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}
;
(d) replaces the integrand by its absolute value;
(e) bounds the integral by the supremum of the integrand;
(f) applies the CBS inequality;
(g) uses the constraint
\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\hat{\mu}}^{(L)}}\leq r
together with
\|\mu^{(L-1)}\|_{\mathcal{S}_{1}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\mu}^{(L-1)}\right),1}=1
;
(h) reparameterizes via the normalized element
\hat{g}^{(L-1)}=g^{(L-1)}/\|g^{(L-1)}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}}
;
(i) enlarges the domain from the unit sphere to the closed unit ball;
(j) follows again from the reproducing property;
(k) uses the symmetry of the unit ball, allowing removal of the absolute value.
∎

Lemma 22

(optimal top-layer representer in BKLs)
.

Fix
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{>0}
, a dataset
\{\mathbf{x}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}\subset\mathcal{X}
, and let

\bm{\varepsilon}=(\varepsilon_{i})_{i=1}^{n}\sim\mathcal{N}^{n}(0,1)
.
Let
u:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
be such that the kernel

\displaystyle k_{u}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}):=k^{(\mathrm{B})}\left(u(\mathbf{x}),u(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\right),\qquad\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X},

is well-defined, and denote by
\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}
the RKHS associated with the Brownian kernel.
Define the kernel matrix
\textbf{K}_{u}^{\textbf{(B)}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
by

\displaystyle\left(\textbf{K}_{u}^{\textbf{(B)}}\right)_{ij}=k^{(\mathrm{B})}\left(u(\mathbf{x}_{i}),u(\mathbf{x}_{j})\right),\qquad i,j\in[n].

Then

\displaystyle\sup_{\|g\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}}\leq 1}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}\,g\left(u(\mathbf{x}_{i})\right)=\frac{1}{n}\sqrt{\bm{\varepsilon}^{\top}\textbf{K}_{u}^{\textbf{(B)}}\bm{\varepsilon}}.

(90)

Moreover, if
\bm{\varepsilon}^{\top}\textbf{K}_{u}^{\textbf{(B)}}\bm{\varepsilon}>0
, the supremum is attained at

\displaystyle g^{\star}=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}\,k^{(\mathrm{B})}(\cdot,u(\mathbf{x}_{i}))}{\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}\,k^{(\mathrm{B})}(\cdot,u(\mathbf{x}_{i}))\right\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}}}.

Proof.

Fix
u
and write
z_{i}:=u(\mathbf{x}_{i})\in\mathbb{R}
for
i\in[n]
. For any
g\in\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}\,g(z_{i})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\left\langle g,\ \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}\,k^{(\mathrm{B})}(\cdot,z_{i})\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\leq}}\|g\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}}\cdot\left\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}\,k^{(\mathrm{B})}(\cdot,z_{i})\right\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}}.

Here (a) uses linearity of the inner product, and (b) applies the CSB inequality. Taking the supremum over
\|g\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}}\leq 1
yields

\displaystyle\sup_{\|g\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}}\leq 1}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}\,g(z_{i})=\frac{1}{n}\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}\,k^{(\mathrm{B})}(\cdot,z_{i})\right\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}},

and, if
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}\,k^{(\mathrm{B})}(\cdot,z_{i})\neq 0
, the supremum is attained at

\displaystyle g^{\star}=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}\,k^{(\mathrm{B})}(\cdot,z_{i})}{\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}\,k^{(\mathrm{B})}(\cdot,z_{i})\right\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}}}.

Finally,

\displaystyle\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}\,k^{(\mathrm{B})}(\cdot,z_{i})\right\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}}^{2}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\left\langle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}\,k^{(\mathrm{B})}(\cdot,z_{i}),\ \sum_{j=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{j}\,k^{(\mathrm{B})}(\cdot,z_{j})\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}\varepsilon_{j}\,\left\langle k^{(\mathrm{B})}(\cdot,z_{i}),k^{(\mathrm{B})}(\cdot,z_{j})\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{=}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}\varepsilon_{j}\,k^{(\mathrm{B})}(z_{i},z_{j})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{=}}\bm{\varepsilon}^{\top}\textbf{K}_{u}^{\textbf{(B)}}\bm{\varepsilon}.

Here (a) expands the norm, (b) uses bilinearity, (c) follows from the reproducing property, and (d) is the definition of the kernel matrix. Substituting yields (
90
).
∎

Lemma 23

(Brownian RKHS characterization)
.

The RKHS
\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}
associated with the Brownian kernel

\displaystyle k^{(\mathrm{B})}(x,x^{\prime})=\frac{|x|+|x^{\prime}|-|x-x^{\prime}|}{2},\qquad\text{for all }x,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}.

consists of all absolutely continuous functions
h:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
such that
h(0)=0
and

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}|h^{\prime}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t<\infty,

with norm

\displaystyle\|h\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}}^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}|h^{\prime}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t.

Proof.

We prove that the RKHS associated with
k^{(\mathrm{B})}
is exactly the Cameron–Martin space on
\mathbb{R}
.

Step-1
(feature representation of
k^{(\mathrm{B})}
):
For
x\in\mathbb{R}
, define the function
\phi_{x}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
by

\displaystyle\phi_{x}\left(t\right)\coloneqq\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mathds{1}_{\left[0,x\right]}\left(t\right),&&x\geq 0,\\
&-\mathds{1}_{\left[x,0\right]}\left(t\right),&&x<0.\end{aligned}\right.

We claim that

\displaystyle k^{(\mathrm{B})}\left(x,x^{\prime}\right)=\left\langle\phi_{x},\phi_{x^{\prime}}\right\rangle_{L^{2}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)},\qquad x,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}.

(91)

To verify this, we distinguish the possible signs of
x
and
x^{\prime}
. If
x\geq 0
and
x^{\prime}\geq 0
, then

\displaystyle\left\langle\phi_{x},\phi_{x^{\prime}}\right\rangle_{L^{2}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathds{1}_{\left[0,x\right]}\left(t\right)\mathds{1}_{\left[0,x^{\prime}\right]}\left(t\right)\mathrm{d}t\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}\left|\left[0,x\right]\cap\left[0,x^{\prime}\right]\right|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{=}}\min\left\{x,x^{\prime}\right\}.

Here (a) follows from the definition of
\phi_{x}
and
\phi_{x^{\prime}}
, (b) uses that the integral of an indicator is the Lebesgue measure of the underlying set, and (c) is immediate since both intervals start at
0
. On the other hand,

\displaystyle k^{(\mathrm{B})}\left(x,x^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\frac{x+x^{\prime}-\left|x-x^{\prime}\right|}{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}\min\left\{x,x^{\prime}\right\},

where (a) uses
|x|=x
and
|x^{\prime}|=x^{\prime}
, and (b) is the elementary identity

\displaystyle\min\left\{a,b\right\}=\frac{a+b-\left|a-b\right|}{2},\qquad a,b\in\mathbb{R}.

If
x<0
and
x^{\prime}<0
, then

\displaystyle\left\langle\phi_{x},\phi_{x^{\prime}}\right\rangle_{L^{2}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathds{1}_{\left[x,0\right]}\left(t\right)\mathds{1}_{\left[x^{\prime},0\right]}\left(t\right)\mathrm{d}t\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}\left|\left[x,0\right]\cap\left[x^{\prime},0\right]\right|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{=}}\min\left\{\left|x\right|,\left|x^{\prime}\right|\right\}.

Here (a) uses that the two minus signs cancel, (b) is again the integral-of-indicator identity, and (c) follows since both intervals end at
0
. Moreover,

\displaystyle k^{(\mathrm{B})}\left(x,x^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\frac{\left|x\right|+\left|x^{\prime}\right|-\left|x-x^{\prime}\right|}{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}\min\left\{\left|x\right|,\left|x^{\prime}\right|\right\},

where (a) is the definition of
k^{(\mathrm{B})}
, and (b) is the same elementary identity applied to
\left|x\right|
and
\left|x^{\prime}\right|
. If
x\geq 0
and
x^{\prime}<0
, or vice versa, then the supports of
\phi_{x}
and
\phi_{x^{\prime}}
are disjoint up to the single point
0
, hence

\displaystyle\left\langle\phi_{x},\phi_{x^{\prime}}\right\rangle_{L^{2}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)}=0.

On the other hand, if
x\geq 0
and
x^{\prime}<0
, then

\displaystyle k^{(\mathrm{B})}\left(x,x^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\frac{x+\left(-x^{\prime}\right)-\left|x-x^{\prime}\right|}{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}\frac{x-x^{\prime}-\left(x-x^{\prime}\right)}{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{=}}0.

Here (a) uses
|x|=x
and
|x^{\prime}|=-x^{\prime}
, (b) uses
x-x^{\prime}>0
, and (c) is immediate. The case
x<0<x^{\prime}
is identical. Thus (
91
) holds for all
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
.

Step-2
(construction of the candidate RKHS):
Let

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}\coloneqq\left\{h:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}:\exists g\in L^{2}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)\text{ such that }h\left(x\right)=\int_{0}^{x}g\left(t\right)\mathrm{d}t\text{ for all }x\in\mathbb{R}\right\}.

For
h\in\mathcal{H}
represented by
g\in L^{2}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)
, define

\displaystyle\left\|h\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}\coloneqq\left\|g\right\|_{L^{2}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)}.

We first check that this is well defined. Suppose

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{x}g\left(t\right)\mathrm{d}t=\int_{0}^{x}\tilde{g}\left(t\right)\mathrm{d}t,\qquad\forall x\in\mathbb{R}.

Then

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{x}\left(g-\tilde{g}\right)\left(t\right)\mathrm{d}t=0,\qquad\forall x\in\mathbb{R}.

Hence the absolutely continuous function

\displaystyle x\mapsto\int_{0}^{x}\left(g-\tilde{g}\right)\left(t\right)\mathrm{d}t

is identically zero, so its derivative vanishes almost everywhere. Therefore,

\displaystyle g=\tilde{g}\qquad\text{a.e. on }\mathbb{R}.

Thus the norm is well defined. Next, for
g\in L^{2}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
, one has

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{x}g\left(t\right)\mathrm{d}t=\left\langle g,\phi_{x}\right\rangle_{L^{2}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)}.

(92)

Indeed, if
x\geq 0
, then

\displaystyle\left\langle g,\phi_{x}\right\rangle_{L^{2}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}g\left(t\right)\mathds{1}_{\left[0,x\right]}\left(t\right)\mathrm{d}t\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}\int_{0}^{x}g\left(t\right)\mathrm{d}t.

Here (a) uses the definition of
\phi_{x}
, and (b) restricts the integral to the support of the indicator. If
x<0
, then

\displaystyle\left\langle g,\phi_{x}\right\rangle_{L^{2}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}-\int_{\mathbb{R}}g\left(t\right)\mathds{1}_{\left[x,0\right]}\left(t\right)\mathrm{d}t\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}-\int_{x}^{0}g\left(t\right)\mathrm{d}t\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{=}}\int_{0}^{x}g\left(t\right)\mathrm{d}t.

Here (a) again uses the definition of
\phi_{x}
, (b) restricts to the support, and (c) is the standard reversal-of-limits identity. Therefore each
h\in\mathcal{H}
satisfies

\displaystyle h\left(x\right)=\left\langle g,\phi_{x}\right\rangle_{L^{2}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)},\qquad x\in\mathbb{R}.

This shows in particular that point evaluation is continuous on
\mathcal{H}
:

\displaystyle\left|h\left(x\right)\right|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\left|\left\langle g,\phi_{x}\right\rangle_{L^{2}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)}\right|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\leq}}\left\|g\right\|_{L^{2}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)}\left\|\phi_{x}\right\|_{L^{2}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)}=\left\|h\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}\sqrt{k^{(\mathrm{B})}\left(x,x\right)}.

Here (a) is (
92
), and (b) follows from the CSB inequality together with

\displaystyle\left\|\phi_{x}\right\|_{L^{2}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)}^{2}=\left\langle\phi_{x},\phi_{x}\right\rangle_{L^{2}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)}=k^{(\mathrm{B})}\left(x,x\right)

by (
91
). Hence
\mathcal{H}
is an RKHS.

Step-3
(identification of the kernel and of the norm):
We now show that the reproducing kernel of
\mathcal{H}
is exactly
k^{(\mathrm{B})}
. Fix
x\in\mathbb{R}
and define

\displaystyle k_{x}\left(\cdot\right)\coloneqq k^{(\mathrm{B})}\left(\cdot,x\right).

By (
91
),

\displaystyle k_{x}\left(y\right)=\left\langle\phi_{y},\phi_{x}\right\rangle_{L^{2}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)},\qquad y\in\mathbb{R}.

Hence
k_{x}\in\mathcal{H}
, namely it is the function associated with
g=\phi_{x}
. Moreover, if
h\in\mathcal{H}
corresponds to
g\in L^{2}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)
, then

\displaystyle\left\langle h,k_{x}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\left\langle g,\phi_{x}\right\rangle_{L^{2}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}h\left(x\right).

Here (a) follows from the definition of the inner product on
\mathcal{H}
, and (b) is (
92
). Thus
k^{(\mathrm{B})}
is the reproducing kernel of
\mathcal{H}
. By uniqueness of the RKHS associated with a given kernel, we conclude that
\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}=\mathcal{H}
. Finally, if
h\in\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}
, then by construction there exists
g\in L^{2}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)
such that

\displaystyle h\left(x\right)=\int_{0}^{x}g\left(t\right)\mathrm{d}t.

Therefore
h
is absolutely continuous on
\mathbb{R}
, satisfies
h\left(0\right)=0
, and has weak derivative

\displaystyle h^{\prime}=g\qquad\text{a.e. on }\mathbb{R}.

Conversely, every absolutely continuous
h:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
with
h\left(0\right)=0
and
h^{\prime}\in L^{2}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)
admits the representation

\displaystyle h\left(x\right)=\int_{0}^{x}h^{\prime}\left(t\right)\mathrm{d}t,\qquad x\in\mathbb{R},

and hence belongs to
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}
. The norm is

\displaystyle\left\|h\right\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k^{(\mathrm{B})}}}^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\left\|g\right\|_{L^{2}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)}^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left|h^{\prime}\left(t\right)\right|^{2}\mathrm{d}t.

Here (a) is the definition of the Hilbert norm on
\mathcal{H}
, and (b) uses
g=h^{\prime}
a.e. This proves the claim.
∎

Lemma 24

(Brownian threshold lemma)
.

For any
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\frac{|a|+|b|-|a-b|}{2}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\mathbf{1}_{\{a\geq t\}}\mathbf{1}_{\{b\geq t\}}+\mathbf{1}_{\{a\leq-t\}}\mathbf{1}_{\{b\leq-t\}}\right)\mathrm{d}t.

(93)

Consequently, for any function
u:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
, if we define the matrix

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}_{u}^{(B)}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n},\qquad\left(\mathbf{K}_{u}^{(B)}\right)_{ij}=\frac{|u(\mathbf{x}_{i})|+|u(\mathbf{x}_{j})|-|u(\mathbf{x}_{i})-u(\mathbf{x}_{j})|}{2},

then

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{\top}\mathbf{K}_{u}^{(B)}\varepsilon=\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\varepsilon\left(A_{t}^{+}(u)\right)^{2}+\varepsilon\left(A_{t}^{-}(u)\right)^{2}\right)\mathrm{d}t,

(94)

where
A_{t}^{\pm}(u):=\{i\in[n]:\pm u(\mathbf{x}_{i})\geq t\}
.

Proof.

We first prove (
93
). Note that the integrand vanishes for all
t>\max(|a|,|b|)
, and hence the integral is finite.

Case 1:
a\geq 0
and
b\geq 0
.

Then

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{\{a\leq-t\}}\mathbf{1}_{\{b\leq-t\}}=0\qquad\text{for all }t\geq 0,

so the right-hand side of (
93
) becomes

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbf{1}_{\{a\geq t\}}\mathbf{1}_{\{b\geq t\}}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbf{1}_{\{t\leq a\}}\mathbf{1}_{\{t\leq b\}}\mathrm{d}t=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbf{1}_{\{t\leq\min(a,b)\}}\mathrm{d}t=\min(a,b).

Since
a,b\geq 0
, we also have

\displaystyle\frac{|a|+|b|-|a-b|}{2}=\frac{a+b-|a-b|}{2}=\min(a,b).

Hence (
93
) holds in this case.

Case 2:
a\leq 0
and
b\leq 0
.

Write

\displaystyle a=-\alpha,\qquad b=-\beta

with
\alpha,\beta\geq 0
. Then

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{\{a\geq t\}}\mathbf{1}_{\{b\geq t\}}=0\qquad\text{for all }t\geq 0,

because
a\geq t
cannot hold for
t>0
when
a\leq 0
. Therefore the right-hand side of
(
93
) becomes

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbf{1}_{\{a\leq-t\}}\mathbf{1}_{\{b\leq-t\}}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbf{1}_{\{\alpha\geq t\}}\mathbf{1}_{\{\beta\geq t\}}\mathrm{d}t=\min(\alpha,\beta).

On the other hand,

\displaystyle\frac{|a|+|b|-|a-b|}{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha+\beta-|(-\alpha)-(-\beta)|}{2}=\frac{\alpha+\beta-|\alpha-\beta|}{2}=\min(\alpha,\beta).

Hence (
93
) holds in this case as well.

Case 3:
ab<0
.

Assume first that
a>0>b
. Then for every
t\geq 0
, at least one factor in each product vanishes:

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{\{b\geq t\}}=0\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathbf{1}_{\{a\leq-t\}}=0,

so both products inside the integral vanish. Thus the right-hand side of
(
93
) is equal to
0
. Moreover, since
a
and
b
have opposite signs,
|a-b|=|a|+|b|
, and therefore the left-hand side is also
\frac{|a|+|b|-|a-b|}{2}=0
. The case
b>0>a
is identical by symmetry. This completes the proof of (
93
). We now prove (
94
). Let

\displaystyle a_{i}:=u(\mathbf{x}_{i}),\qquad i\in[n].

Then,

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{\top}\mathbf{K}_{u}^{(B)}\varepsilon

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}\varepsilon_{j}\frac{|a_{i}|+|a_{j}|-|a_{i}-a_{j}|}{2}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}\varepsilon_{j}\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\mathbf{1}_{\{a_{i}\geq t\}}\mathbf{1}_{\{a_{j}\geq t\}}+\mathbf{1}_{\{a_{i}\leq-t\}}\mathbf{1}_{\{a_{j}\leq-t\}}\right)\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{=}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}\varepsilon_{j}\left(\mathbf{1}_{\{a_{i}\geq t\}}\mathbf{1}_{\{a_{j}\geq t\}}+\mathbf{1}_{\{a_{i}\leq-t\}}\mathbf{1}_{\{a_{j}\leq-t\}}\right)\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{=}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\left[\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}\mathbf{1}_{\{a_{i}\geq t\}}\right)^{2}+\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}\mathbf{1}_{\{a_{i}\leq-t\}}\right)^{2}\right]dt.

Here, (a) follows from the definition of the quadratic form associated with
\mathbf{K}_{u}^{(B)}
, (b) is implied by (
93
), (c) follows from the linearity of the integral, since the sum over
i,j
is finite, and (d) is obtained by factorizing the two double sums into squares.
Moreover,

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}\mathbf{1}_{\{a_{i}\geq t\}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(e)}}{{=}}\left(\sum_{i\in A_{t}^{+}(u)}\varepsilon_{i}\right)^{2}=\varepsilon(A_{t}^{+}(u))^{2},

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}\mathbf{1}_{\{a_{i}\leq-t\}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(f)}}{{=}}\left(\sum_{i\in A_{t}^{-}(u)}\varepsilon_{i}\right)^{2}=\varepsilon(A_{t}^{-}(u))^{2}.

In (e) and (f) we used the definitions of
A_{t}^{\pm}(u)
and
\varepsilon(B)
: the indicators restrict the sums to the index sets
A_{t}^{\pm}(u)
. Hence,

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{\top}\mathbf{K}_{u}^{(B)}\varepsilon=\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big(\varepsilon(A_{t}^{+}(u))^{2}+\varepsilon(A_{t}^{-}(u))^{2}\Big)\mathrm{d}t.

∎

Lemma 25

(graph coarea lemma)
.

For every
\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{TV}_{T}(\mathbf{a})=\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\partial_{T}(A_{t}^{+}(\mathbf{a}))+\partial_{T}(A_{t}^{-}(\mathbf{a}))\right)\mathrm{d}t.

(95)

where
A_{t}^{\pm}(\mathbf{a})=\{i\in[n]:\pm a_{i}\geq t\}
.

Proof.

We first prove the identity edge by edge. Fix an edge
\{i,j\}\in E_{T}
. We claim that

\displaystyle|a_{i}-a_{j}|=\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\big|\mathbf{1}_{\{a_{i}\geq t\}}-\mathbf{1}_{\{a_{j}\geq t\}}\big|+\big|\mathbf{1}_{\{a_{i}\leq-t\}}-\mathbf{1}_{\{a_{j}\leq-t\}}\big|\right)\mathrm{d}t.

(96)

Note that the integrand vanishes for all
t>\max(|a_{i}|,|a_{j}|)
, so the integral is finite. We verify (
96
) by cases.

Case 1:
a_{i}\geq 0
and
a_{j}\geq 0
.

In this case,

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{\{a_{i}\leq-t\}}=\mathbf{1}_{\{a_{j}\leq-t\}}=0\qquad\text{for all }t\geq 0,

so the second term in the integrand vanishes. Hence the right-hand side of
(
96
) becomes

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\big|\mathbf{1}_{\{a_{i}\geq t\}}-\mathbf{1}_{\{a_{j}\geq t\}}\big|\mathrm{d}t.

Assume without loss of generality that
a_{i}\geq a_{j}
. Then the difference is nonnegative for all
t\geq 0
, and

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{\{a_{i}\geq t\}}-\mathbf{1}_{\{a_{j}\geq t\}}=\begin{cases}0,&0\leq t\leq a_{j},\\
1,&a_{j}<t\leq a_{i},\\
0,&t>a_{i}.\end{cases}

Therefore

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\left|\mathbf{1}_{\{a_{i}\geq t\}}-\mathbf{1}_{\{a_{j}\geq t\}}\right|\mathrm{d}t=a_{i}-a_{j}=|a_{i}-a_{j}|.

Case 2:
a_{i}\leq 0
and
a_{j}\leq 0
.

For
t>0
, the first term in the integrand vanishes, and hence it does not contribute to the integral, and we only need the second one. Write

\displaystyle a_{i}=-\alpha,\qquad a_{j}=-\beta

with
\alpha,\beta\geq 0
. Then

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{\{a_{i}\leq-t\}}=\mathbf{1}_{\{\alpha\geq t\}},\qquad\mathbf{1}_{\{a_{j}\leq-t\}}=\mathbf{1}_{\{\beta\geq t\}}.

Hence the right-hand side of (
96
) becomes

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\big|\mathbf{1}_{\{\alpha\geq t\}}-\mathbf{1}_{\{\beta\geq t\}}\big|\mathrm{d}t.

Assume without loss of generality that
\alpha\geq\beta
. Then this integral equals

\displaystyle\alpha-\beta=|(-\alpha)-(-\beta)|=|a_{i}-a_{j}|.

Case 3:
a_{i}\geq 0\geq a_{j}
.

For
t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{\{a_{i}\geq t\}}=\begin{cases}1,&0\leq t\leq a_{i},\\
0,&t>a_{i},\end{cases}\qquad\mathbf{1}_{\{a_{j}\geq t\}}=0,

because
a_{j}\leq 0
. Therefore

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\big|\mathbf{1}_{\{a_{i}\geq t\}}-\mathbf{1}_{\{a_{j}\geq t\}}\big|\mathrm{d}t=a_{i}.

Also,

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{\{a_{i}\leq-t\}}=0,\qquad\mathbf{1}_{\{a_{j}\leq-t\}}=\begin{cases}1,&0\leq t\leq-a_{j},\\
0,&t>-a_{j}.\end{cases}

Hence

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\big|\mathbf{1}_{\{a_{i}\leq-t\}}-\mathbf{1}_{\{a_{j}\leq-t\}}\big|\mathrm{d}t=-a_{j}.

Adding the two contributions gives

\displaystyle a_{i}+(-a_{j})=a_{i}-a_{j}=|a_{i}-a_{j}|.

Case 4:
a_{j}\geq 0\geq a_{i}
.

\displaystyle\sum_{\{i,j\}\in E_{T}}|a_{i}-a_{j}|

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\mathbf{a})}}{{=}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\sum_{\{i,j\}\in E_{T}}\left(\left|\mathbf{1}_{\{a_{i}\geq t\}}-\mathbf{1}_{\{a_{j}\geq t\}}\right|+\left|\mathbf{1}_{\{a_{i}\leq-t\}}-\mathbf{1}_{\{a_{j}\leq-t\}}\right|\right)\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\partial_{T}(A_{t}^{+}(\mathbf{a}))+\partial_{T}(A_{t}^{-}(\mathbf{a}))\right)\mathrm{d}t.

Here, (a) holds by summing the edgewise identity (
96
) over all edges
\{i,j\}\in E_{T}
and exchanging the finite sum and the integral by linearity, (b) follows from the definition of
\partial_{T}(B)
, applied with
B=A_{t}^{+}(\mathbf{a})
and
B=A_{t}^{-}(\mathbf{a})
.
This is exactly (
95
).
∎

Lemma 26

(boundary counting lemma)
.

Let
n\geq 2
, let
T=(V,E_{T})
be a tree on the vertex set
[n]
. For every integer
m\in\{0,1,\dots,n-1\}
,

\displaystyle\left|\left\{B\subset[n]:\partial_{T}(B)\leq m\right\}\right|\leq 2\sum_{k=0}^{m}\binom{n-1}{k}.

(97)

where
\partial_{T}(B)=\sum_{\{i,j\}\in E_{T}}\left|\mathbf{1}_{B}(i)-\mathbf{1}_{B}(j)\right|
.

Proof.

For each set
B\subset[n]
, define its set of cut edges by

\displaystyle F(B):=\left\{\{i,j\}\in E_{T}:\mathbf{1}_{B}(i)\neq\mathbf{1}_{B}(j)\right\}.

By definition of
\partial_{T}(B)
,
|F(B)|=\partial_{T}(B)
. Fix
F\subset E_{T}
. We show that there are at most two sets
B\subset[n]
such that
F(B)=F
. Remove the edges in
F
from the tree
T
. Since
T
is a tree, the graph
T\setminus F
is a forest. Let its connected components be
C_{1},\dots,C_{r}
. If
F(B)=F
, then for every edge
\{i,j\}\in E_{T}\setminus F
one has
\mathbf{1}_{B}(i)=\mathbf{1}_{B}(j)
, hence
\mathbf{1}_{B}
is constant on each component
C_{\ell}
. Contract each component
C_{\ell}
to a single vertex. Since
T
is a tree, this contraction preserves connectivity and acyclicity, hence the resulting graph is a tree with edge set
F
. Along every edge of this contracted tree, the indicator must flip, because these are exactly the cut edges. Therefore, once the value of
\mathbf{1}_{B}
is fixed on one contracted vertex, all other values are uniquely determined by alternation along paths. There are exactly two choices for the initial value, namely
0
or
1
. Hence there are at most two sets
B
such that
F(B)=F
. We now count. If
\partial_{T}(B)\leq m
, then
|F(B)|\leq m
. Since
|E_{T}|=n-1
, the number of subsets
F\subset E_{T}
with
|F|=k
is
\binom{n-1}{k}
. Therefore, since each
F
can correspond to at most two sets
B
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\left\{B\subset[n]:\partial_{T}(B)\leq m\right\}\right|\leq 2\sum_{k=0}^{m}\binom{n-1}{k},

∎

Lemma 27

(tree scan lemma)
.

Let
n\geq 2
, let
T=(V,E_{T})
be a tree on the vertex set
[n]
, and let
\bm{\varepsilon}=(\varepsilon_{1},\dots,\varepsilon_{n})\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)^{n}
. There exists a universal constant
C>0
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{N}^{n}(0,1)}\left[\sup_{B\subset[n]}\frac{\varepsilon(B)^{2}}{\partial_{T}(B)\vee 1}\right]\leq C\,n\ln(2n),

(98)

where

\displaystyle\partial_{T}(B)=\sum_{\{i,j\}\in E_{T}}\left|\mathbf{1}_{B}(i)-\mathbf{1}_{B}(j)\right|,\quad\text{and}\quad\varepsilon(B)=\sum_{i\in B}\varepsilon_{i},\qquad\text{for any }B\subset[n].

Proof.

We divide the proof into six steps.

Step-1
(dyadic decomposition of the boundary size):
For
j\geq 0
, define

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{j}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{B\subseteq[n]:\partial_{T}\left(B\right)\leq 2^{j}\right\},

\displaystyle Y_{j}

\displaystyle\coloneqq 2^{-j}\sup_{B\in\mathcal{B}_{j}}\varepsilon\left(B\right)^{2}.

Let

\displaystyle J\coloneqq\left\lceil\log_{2}\left(n-1\right)\right\rceil.

Since
\partial_{T}\left(B\right)\leq n-1
for all
B\subseteq[n]
, it is enough to consider
j\in\left\{0,\dots,J\right\}
. We claim that

\displaystyle\sup_{B\subseteq[n]}\frac{\varepsilon\left(B\right)^{2}}{\partial_{T}\left(B\right)\vee 1}\leq 2\max_{0\leq j\leq J}Y_{j}.

(99)

To see this, fix
B\subseteq[n]
and define

\displaystyle k\coloneqq\partial_{T}\left(B\right)\vee 1.

Let
j
be the smallest index such that
k\leq 2^{j}
. If
j=0
, then
k=1
, and thus

\displaystyle\frac{\varepsilon\left(B\right)^{2}}{\partial_{T}\left(B\right)\vee 1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\varepsilon\left(B\right)^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\leq}}\sup_{A\in\mathcal{B}_{0}}\varepsilon\left(A\right)^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{=}}Y_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{\leq}}2Y_{0}.

Here (a) uses
k=1
, (b) follows because
B\in\mathcal{B}_{0}
, (c) is the definition of
Y_{0}
, and (d) is trivial. If
j\geq 1
, then
2^{j-1}<k\leq 2^{j}
, and
B\in\mathcal{B}_{j}
. Hence

\displaystyle\varepsilon\left(B\right)^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\leq}}\sup_{A\in\mathcal{B}_{j}}\varepsilon\left(A\right)^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}2^{j}Y_{j},

where (a) uses
B\in\mathcal{B}_{j}
and (b) is the definition of
Y_{j}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\frac{\varepsilon\left(B\right)^{2}}{k}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\leq}}\frac{2^{j}Y_{j}}{2^{j-1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}2Y_{j}.

Here (a) follows from
k>2^{j-1}
, and (b) is a simplification. Taking the supremum over
B
proves (
99
).

Step-2
(cardinality bound for each dyadic class):
Fix
j\in\left\{0,\dots,J\right\}
and define

\displaystyle m_{j}\coloneqq\min\left(2^{j},n-1\right).

Since always
\partial_{T}\left(B\right)\leq n-1
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{j}=\left\{B\subseteq[n]:\partial_{T}\left(B\right)\leq m_{j}\right\}.

Hence, by
\Cref
lem:boundary-counting-rigorous,

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{B}_{j}\right|\leq 2\sum_{k=0}^{m_{j}}\binom{n-1}{k}.

(100)

We now show that there exists a universal constant
c_{0}>0
such that

\displaystyle\ln\left|\mathcal{B}_{j}\right|\leq c_{0}\,2^{j}\ln\left(2n\right).

(101)

Set
m\coloneqq m_{j}
. We distinguish two cases.

Case A:
1\leq m\leq\left(n-1\right)/2
.

Then

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{m}\binom{n-1}{k}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\leq}}\left(m+1\right)\binom{n-1}{m}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\leq}}\left(m+1\right)\left(\frac{e\left(n-1\right)}{m}\right)^{m}.

Here (a) uses that
k\mapsto\binom{n-1}{k}
is increasing for
k\leq\left(n-1\right)/2
, and (b) is the standard bound

\displaystyle\binom{N}{m}\leq\left(\frac{eN}{m}\right)^{m}.

Combining this with (
100
), we get

\displaystyle\ln\left|\mathcal{B}_{j}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\ln 2+\ln\left(m+1\right)+m\ln\left(\frac{e\left(n-1\right)}{m}\right).

Since
m\geq 1
, we have
\ln\left(m+1\right)\leq m\ln 2
, and since
n-1\leq n\leq 2n
, it follows that

\displaystyle\ln\left|\mathcal{B}_{j}\right|\leq c_{1}\,m\ln\left(2n\right)\leq c_{1}\,2^{j}\ln\left(2n\right).

Case B:
m>\left(n-1\right)/2
.

Then

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{m}\binom{n-1}{k}\leq 2^{n-1},

and therefore
\ln\left|\mathcal{B}_{j}\right|\leq n\ln 2
. Since
m>\left(n-1\right)/2
, one has
n<2m+1\leq 3m
, and thus

\displaystyle\ln\left|\mathcal{B}_{j}\right|\leq c_{2}\,m\ln\left(2n\right)\leq c_{2}\,2^{j}\ln\left(2n\right).

Combining the two cases proves (
101
).

Step-3
(tail bound for
Y_{j}
):
Fix
j\in\left\{0,\dots,J\right\}
and let
t>0
. Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(Y_{j}\geq t\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{B\in\mathcal{B}_{j}}\varepsilon\left(B\right)^{2}\geq 2^{j}t\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{B\in\mathcal{B}_{j}}\left|\varepsilon\left(B\right)\right|\geq\sqrt{2^{j}t}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{\leq}}\sum_{B\in\mathcal{B}_{j}}\mathbb{P}\left(\left|\varepsilon\left(B\right)\right|\geq\sqrt{2^{j}t}\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{\leq}}2\left|\mathcal{B}_{j}\right|\exp\left(-\frac{2^{j}t}{2n}\right).

Here (a) is the definition of
Y_{j}
, (b) uses the equivalence between
\varepsilon\left(B\right)^{2}\geq 2^{j}t
and
\left|\varepsilon\left(B\right)\right|\geq\sqrt{2^{j}t}
, (c) is the union bound, and (d) follows from the Gaussian tail estimate together with

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{N}^{n}(0,1)}\left[\varepsilon\left(B\right)^{2}\right]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\sum_{i\in B}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{N}^{n}(0,1)}\left[\varepsilon_{i}^{2}\right]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}\left|B\right|\leq n.

Here (a) follows from independence and zero mean of the
\varepsilon_{i}
, and (b) uses
\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{N}^{n}(0,1)}[\varepsilon_{i}^{2}]=1
. Using (
101
), we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(Y_{j}\geq t\right)\leq 2\exp\left(c_{0}\,2^{j}\ln\left(2n\right)-\frac{2^{j}t}{2n}\right).

(102)

Step-4
(uniform tail estimate):
Define

\displaystyle t_{0}

\displaystyle\coloneqq 4\left(c_{0}+1\right)n\ln\left(2n\right),

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle\coloneqq t_{0}+2nu,\qquad u\geq 0.

Substituting this choice into (
102
), we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(Y_{j}\geq t\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2\exp\left(c_{0}\,2^{j}\ln\left(2n\right)-\frac{2^{j}}{2n}\left(t_{0}+2nu\right)\right)=2\exp\left(c_{0}\,2^{j}\ln\left(2n\right)-\frac{2^{j}t_{0}}{2n}-2^{j}u\right).

Since
t_{0}/2n=2\left(c_{0}+1\right)\ln\left(2n\right)
, it follows that

\displaystyle c_{0}\,2^{j}\ln\left(2n\right)-\frac{2^{j}t_{0}}{2n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\left(c_{0}-2\left(c_{0}+1\right)\right)2^{j}\ln\left(2n\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}-\left(c_{0}+2\right)2^{j}\ln\left(2n\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{\leq}}0.

Here (a) follows by substitution, (b) by simplification, and (c) since the coefficient is negative. Hence

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(Y_{j}\geq t_{0}+2nu\right)\leq 2e^{-2^{j}u}\leq 2e^{-u},

(103)

where the last inequality uses
2^{j}\geq 1
.

Step-5
(maximum over the dyadic levels):
By the union bound and (
103
),

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{0\leq j\leq J}Y_{j}\geq t_{0}+2nu\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\leq}}\sum_{j=0}^{J}\mathbb{P}\left(Y_{j}\geq t_{0}+2nu\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\leq}}2\left(J+1\right)e^{-u}.

Here (a) is the union bound and (b) uses (
103
). Setting

\displaystyle u\coloneqq v+\ln\left(2\left(J+1\right)\right),\qquad v\geq 0,

we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{0\leq j\leq J}Y_{j}\geq t_{0}+2n\ln\left(2\left(J+1\right)\right)+2nv\right)\leq e^{-v}.

(104)

Step-6
(expectation bound):
From (
104
), a standard tail integration argument gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{N}^{n}(0,1)}\left[\max_{0\leq j\leq J}Y_{j}\right]\leq t_{0}+2n\ln\left(2\left(J+1\right)\right)+2n.

Since

\displaystyle J\leq\log_{2}\left(n-1\right)+1\leq\log_{2}n+1,

we have

\displaystyle\ln\left(2\left(J+1\right)\right)\leq 2\ln\left(2n\right),

and therefore

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{N}^{n}(0,1)}\left[\max_{0\leq j\leq J}Y_{j}\right]\leq C_{1}\,n\ln\left(2n\right)

(105)

for some universal constant
C_{1}>0
. Finally, taking expectations in (
99
) and applying (
105
), we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{N}^{n}(0,1)}\left[\sup_{B\subseteq[n]}\frac{\varepsilon\left(B\right)^{2}}{\partial_{T}\left(B\right)\vee 1}\right]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\leq}}2\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{N}^{n}(0,1)}\left[\max_{0\leq j\leq J}Y_{j}\right]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\leq}}2C_{1}\,n\ln\left(2n\right).

Here (a) follows from (
99
), and (b) uses (
105
). This proves (
98
).
∎

Lemma 28

(coarea identity with boundary correction)
.

Let
n\geq 2
, let
T=(V,E_{T})
be a tree on the vertex set
[n]
, and let
\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Then

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\left((\partial_{T}(A_{t}^{+}(\mathbf{a}))\vee 1)+(\partial_{T}(A_{t}^{-}(\mathbf{a}))\vee 1)\right)\mathrm{d}t\leq\sum_{\{i,j\}\in E_{T}}|a_{i}-a_{j}|+2\max_{i\in[n]}|a_{i}|.

(106)

where
A_{t}^{\pm}(u)=\{i\in[n]:\pm u(\mathbf{x}_{i})\geq t\}
.

Proof.

We argue in three steps.

Step-1
(pointwise reduction):
For any subset
B\subseteq[n]
, one has

\displaystyle\partial_{T}\left(B\right)\vee 1\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\leq}}\partial_{T}\left(B\right)+\mathds{1}_{\left\{\partial_{T}\left(B\right)=0\right\}}.

(107)

Here (a) holds because if
\partial_{T}\left(B\right)\geq 1
, then the left-hand side equals
\partial_{T}\left(B\right)
, whereas if
\partial_{T}\left(B\right)=0
, then the left-hand side equals
1
and the right-hand side also equals
1
. Since
T
is a tree, the condition
\partial_{T}\left(B\right)=0
is equivalent to saying that no edge of
T
connects
B
to its complement. As
T
is connected, this happens if and only if either
B=\emptyset
or
B=[n]
. Applying (
107
) twice to
B=A_{t}^{+}\left(\mathbf{a}\right)
and
B=A_{t}^{-}\left(\mathbf{a}\right)
and adding the two inequalities, we obtain

\displaystyle\left(\partial_{T}\left(A_{t}^{+}\left(\mathbf{a}\right)\right)\vee 1\right)+\left(\partial_{T}\left(A_{t}^{-}\left(\mathbf{a}\right)\right)\vee 1\right)

\displaystyle\leq\partial_{T}\left(A_{t}^{+}\left(\mathbf{a}\right)\right)+\mathds{1}_{\left\{\partial_{T}\left(A_{t}^{+}\left(\mathbf{a}\right)\right)=0\right\}}+\partial_{T}\left(A_{t}^{-}\left(\mathbf{a}\right)\right)+\mathds{1}_{\left\{\partial_{T}\left(A_{t}^{-}\left(\mathbf{a}\right)\right)=0\right\}}.

(108)

Step-2
(integration and use of the coarea identity):
Integrating the pointwise bound in (
108
) over
t\in\left[0,\infty\right)
gives

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\left(\partial_{T}\left(A_{t}^{+}\left(\mathbf{a}\right)\right)\vee 1\right)+\left(\partial_{T}\left(A_{t}^{-}\left(\mathbf{a}\right)\right)\vee 1\right)\right)\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\partial_{T}\left(A_{t}^{+}\left(\mathbf{a}\right)\right)+\partial_{T}\left(A_{t}^{-}\left(\mathbf{a}\right)\right)\right)\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathds{1}_{\left\{\partial_{T}\left(A_{t}^{+}\left(\mathbf{a}\right)\right)=0\right\}}\mathrm{d}t+\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathds{1}_{\left\{\partial_{T}\left(A_{t}^{-}\left(\mathbf{a}\right)\right)=0\right\}}\mathrm{d}t.

(109)

Next, by the graph coarea lemma, we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\partial_{T}\left(A_{t}^{+}\left(\mathbf{a}\right)\right)+\partial_{T}\left(A_{t}^{-}\left(\mathbf{a}\right)\right)\right)\mathrm{d}t=\sum_{\left\{i,j\right\}\in E_{T}}\left|a_{i}-a_{j}\right|.

(110)

This identity gives the main term on the right-hand side.

Step-3
(control of the correction terms):
We now estimate the remaining integrals. For the positive level sets, by definition,

\displaystyle A_{t}^{+}\left(\mathbf{a}\right)=\emptyset\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}a_{i}<t\ \forall i\in[n]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}t>\max_{i\in[n]}a_{i},
